Hexatronic · HTRO-SE · the new Memo conveyor's card of 22 October 2026

What the Investment Analyst wrote in the layers behind the Hexatronic memo

These are the Investment Analyst's answers to the 101 questions it worked through before the Stock-specific Investment Case A&J Memo on Hexatronic was written: 46,536 words, against 12,858 words in the memo Analyst's draft.

Word for word. Every answer below is the Investment Analyst's text exactly as written. Two things are changed, both layout only: each group's judgement sits above the questions it sums up, because it was written last; and each score is shown as a pill. A script checked that every word of every answer is on this page, in order.

The Investment Analyst's terms: it calls Layer 1 "the first set", Layer 2 "the second set", and Layer 3 and Layer 4 "the third and fourth sets". "The Consumer" is the Investment Analyst who works the next layer. "Our" means our own forecasts, as opposed to the sell-side's. Acronyms are explained under "Terms used" at the end, and on a computer by hovering over a dotted term.

On request: a clarity-edited version, with a summary of the most important messages and a comparison with the memo, as made for Xvivo Perfusion on 30 October 2026.

How to read the scores

The Investment Analyst ended each answer with a score from 1 to 100: 100 is the most positive for an investment case, 1 the most negative, and 50 neither. What "positive" refers to differs by layer, so each layer states its own meaning below its title.

The letter beside each score is the system's own grade of that score (A from 90, B from 75, C from 50, D from 25, F below 25). ❌ marks a D, ❌❌ an F.

Layer 1

Assessing the foundational components of an investment case

82 questions in 10 groups · 20,869 words · written 23 October 2026, 11:27 to 11:45 UK, Opus, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to investigate and research each question, analyse the relevant information and form an insightful judgement regarding it, and to do the same for each group of questions as a whole, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating model and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Hexatronic (HTRO-SE): First set of questions, foundational components of the investment case

Prepared 22-23 October 2026 for the Investment Analyst who builds the operating and financial model (second set).

Reading notes for the Consumer (read before the answers)

  • Source check on the latest Earnings Review. It covers Q3 2026 (1 July to 30 September 2026), released 07:00 CEST on 22 October 2026; its own §4 confirms this. It therefore supersedes the Q2 2026 review and is one quarter newer than the FactSet financials block, whose last reported period is Q2 2026 and whose consensus is in substance pre-Q3.
  • Consensus is thin. FactSet shows one broker by name (Redeye: FY2026-28 EPS SEK 2.16 / 2.71 / 3.23; revenue SEK 8,449m / 9,439m / 10,008m). The FactSet median is SEK 2.30 / 2.71 / 3.23 / 4.18 (FY2029 one broker). The company-commissioned Modular Finance consensus has four to five contributors. Write "the covering broker" or "the four-contributor consensus", not "the market", where it matters.
  • Bases. The operating line that the company, Redeye and Modular Finance use is adjusted EBITA (before items affecting comparability and before amortisation of acquired intangibles). FactSet "operating EBITDA" (FY2025 SEK 476m) includes one-offs; FactSet "brokers' basis" EBITDA FY2025 was SEK 804m (10.7%). FY2025 revenue is SEK 7,519m (company), 7,245m (FactSet fundamentals), 7,543m (brokers' basis). Never compute growth from the FactSet history table to the consensus table.
  • A data error to avoid. The Q2 2026 review's Q1 2026 figures (adjusted EBITA SEK 61m, margin 3.6%, organic -10%) contradict every other source. Use Q1 2026 adjusted EBITA SEK 146m (8.6%), group organic -2%, Fiber Solutions organic -11%.
  • Quarterly spine (company basis).

  • Q3 2026 segments: Fiber Solutions SEK 1,414m (+14%, organic +13%), adjusted EBITA SEK 133m (9.4%); Data Center SEK 692m (+107%, organic +44%), SEK 117m (16.9%); Harsh Environment SEK 393m (+25%, organic +6%), SEK 43m (11.0%). Q3 group growth of 33% = 17% organic + 14% acquired + 1% currency. North America 48% of sales (SEK 1,190m, +80% year on year), Europe 43% (Fiber Solutions Europe -9%), Asia-Pacific 9% (+37%).
  • FY2025 segments: Fiber Solutions SEK 4,870m (organic -5%, 6.4% margin); Data Center SEK 1,409m (organic +37%, SEK 252m, 17.9%); Harsh Environment SEK 1,241m (organic +11%, 11.2%). Group organic +3%, acquired +2%, currency -5%. Group adjusted EBITA: 2022 16.6% margin; 2023 SEK 1,234m (15.1%); 2024 SEK 803m (10.6%); 2025 SEK 632m (8.4%).
  • Scores are 1 to 100; higher is more positive for an investment case in Hexatronic.

External change forces

C6510 questions
C-0168C-0250C-0362C-0458C-0535C-0675C-0740C-0872C-0955C-1066

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The external environment has flipped from a net headwind in 2024 to mid-2025 into the strongest net tailwind since the 2022 boom, and it is acting on Hexatronic through two engines that did not exist in its last cycle: AI data-centre fibre (structural, about 28% of Q3 sales) and a US fibre rebuild (cyclical, with a BEAD tail), now 48% of sales by geography. The headwinds are equally clear and differently shaped: Europe's FTTH market is structurally smaller, glass supply is concentrated in the hands of competitors and has become a cap on volume, and the krona and the discount rate act at the company level. Three conclusions matter for the model. First, Q3 2026 is close to the peak rate of change: Data Center normalises in Q4, comparisons harden from Q2 2027, and supply binds, so 2027 organic growth should be modelled well below 2026's second-half pace. Second, the upside is capped by supply while the downside (US refill ending; a financing-led hyperscaler pause) is open, so the distribution of 2027 outcomes is skewed modestly to the downside around a still-positive base. Third, the tailwinds are front-loaded into 2026-28 and competitive capacity arrives from 2027-28, so margin and growth assumptions for 2029 should be more conservative than for 2027.

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, compared with the last 12 to 18 months.

C-01

To what extent is end-market demand changing, structurally or cyclically, and with how much visibility? (A cyclical swing counts when it has two-plus years of visibility.)

C68

Judgement. End-market demand has swung from a net contraction (2024 to mid-2025) to the strongest net expansion since 2022, but it is three separate cycles of different character, and only one of them has genuine multi-year visibility. The modeller should not treat Q3 2026's 17% organic growth as a run-rate: the US fibre leg is a cyclical rebound that may partly be refilling of depleted stock, the data-centre leg is structural but lumpy and was flattered by timing in Q3, and the European leg is structurally shrinking.

  • US fibre (FTTH, transport and middle-mile): cyclical rebound, strong, visibility of roughly 12 to 24 months. North American Fiber Solutions grew 44% in Q2 2026; North American group sales grew 80% in Q3 to SEK 1,190m, and management reversed its decision to downsize the Clinton, South Carolina plant. Drivers: private-equity-backed tier-2/3 builders restarting (management says about 80% of US fibre construction is privately funded), middle-mile and long-haul links to data centres (the Viper Core transport product), and BEAD from 2027 (state contracts now being signed with four-year construction windows, which gives a subsidised tail into 2030). Corroboration is mixed: Dura-Line +23% (Q1 2026) and Prysmian Digital Solutions +18% organic support an industry-wide upturn, while Clearfield cut its FY26 outlook because rural providers delayed builds. Hexatronic's exposure is skewed to the stronger part (transport, PE-backed metro builders, direct sales), not the weak rural/distributor part.
  • The "use, refill or price" test (Wisdom Library) has not been passed. After an 18-to-24-month destock, some of the US surge is probably customers refilling. Hexatronic's direct model lowers this risk relative to distributor-led peers, but the company discloses no order intake, book-to-bill or channel data, and the order book is only about four months of sales. Price is also now contributing (pass-through of fibre and transport costs). The durable component is use; the model should assume US Fiber Solutions growth decelerates from the Q2-Q3 2026 pace once refill ends and comparisons harden from Q2 2027.
  • Data centre: structural, the clearest multi-year force. Data Center organic growth was 37% in 2025, 20% in Q1, 27% in Q2 and 44% in Q3 2026. Industry evidence: data-centre optical-cable demand about 20% a year 2025-31 against about 7% for the overall market (CRU via Jefferies), fibre counts per connection rising from 2 to 12-16 cores, hyperscaler capex roughly USD 600bn (2025) to USD 1.1tn (2026). Management itself says Q3 benefited from seasonality and project timing and that Q4 will normalise toward the first-half pattern. Revenue is project and installation work, so quarterly conversion is uneven (data-centre projects slip up to six months on equipment lead times and owner indecision).
  • Europe FTTH: structural maturity, no recovery inside the horizon. Fiber Solutions Europe fell 31% (Q1), 10% (Q2) and 9% (Q3 2026); the UK and DACH are weakest; the market has moved from homes passed to homes connected (a smaller market that favours drop and connection products). Sweden's 30% decline since its 2017 peak is the base rate. Fiscal support (German Gigabit Strategy, UK Project Gigabit) has not transmitted into demand.
  • Harsh Environment: defence structural and strong; oil and gas cyclical and weak (dynamic cables subdued; management expects the same in Q4).
  • Change over the horizon. Last 6 months versus the prior 12-18: decisive positive inflection (group organic -2% to +11% to +17%). Next 6 months: Q4 and Q1 are seasonally lower, Data Center normalises, and Q1 2027 meets a very easy comparison (Q1 2026 SEK 1,698m, cold winter). Year-on-year growth stays positive; the rate of change probably peaked in Q3 2026.
  • Implication for the model. Assume group organic growth falls from 17% (Q3 2026) to high single digits to low double digits in 2027: Data Center 15-20% organic on a normalised base, US Fiber Solutions mid-to-high single digits after the rebound, Fiber Solutions Europe flat to -5%, Harsh Environment mid-to-high single digits. Visibility is roughly 12 months for the group, longer for data-centre thematics and the BEAD tail.
C-02

To what extent, and in which direction, is competitive intensity changing?

C50

Judgement. Competitive intensity is diverging by region and by time: it is easing now in the United States and in data-centre supply because scarcity of glass and of compliant local capacity has removed price competition, it remains intense and structural in European ducts, and it is set to tighten across the board from 2027-28 as the larger integrated competitors' capacity lands.

  • United States, easing (next 6-12 months). The industry raised prices together in 2026; Build America Buy America (BABA) and tariffs exclude imports (less than 5% of Hexatronic's sales face tariffs); locally made duct capacity is scarce enough that Hexatronic reversed its Clinton downsizing. In 2024-25 the same market saw lower year-on-year duct pricing offset by volume, so the current benign state is cycle-dependent.
  • Europe, intense and unchanged. Microduct overcapacity from the 2022 boom, distressed inventory sales and "renewed price wars"; specialised extrusion equipment has low liquidation value, so capacity does not exit (competitive intensity is entry minus exit). Chinese fibre exports depressed European pricing.
  • Data centre, rising underneath the shortage. Corning wins an estimated 50-60% of optical-communications bids and is gaining 2-3 points of share (expert, September 2026); Amphenol completed its USD 10.5bn purchase of CommScope's connectivity and cable business in 2026; Prysmian is adding 40-50% US fibre capacity (commercial from H2 2028) and signing direct hyperscaler deals; private-equity installer platforms (Wize Solutions doubled by acquisition, October 2026) compete for both work and Hexatronic's acquisition targets. Hyperscaler and defence buyers currently prioritise supply over price (Jefferies, October 2026), which suppresses price competition while the shortage lasts.
  • Direction over the horizon. Last 6 months versus the prior 12-18: easing in the US, unchanged in Europe. Beyond 18 months: tightening, as roughly 230m fibre-km of new Chinese preform capacity (by end-2027), Corning's 50% US fibre expansion and Prysmian's US expansion arrive into a market whose data-centre growth is expected to slow from 2028.
  • Implication for the model. Do not extrapolate 2026 US pricing beyond 2027; model European pricing flat to down with volume; treat data-centre price realisation as supported until about 2028 and at risk thereafter.
C-03

To what extent is a disruption force strengthening or being disproved, and in which direction does it act on the company?

C62

Judgement. The disruption forces acting on fibre are, on balance, being disproved where Hexatronic earns money and are acting in its favour over the next two to three years, because AI data-centre architecture is substituting optics for copper and raising fibre content per site; the genuine disruption risk is a slower value migration away from conventional cable and assembly toward precision components, standards and direct hyperscaler procurement, where Hexatronic has no intellectual property.

  • Disproved or contained. Fixed wireless and low-earth-orbit satellite are confined to sparse rural areas by throughput, cost and symmetry; LEO constellations themselves need fibre backhaul. The one place they gained is BEAD: the June 2025 redesign removed the fibre-first rule, saving about USD 21bn, and alternatives can win up to 26% of funds. That dilutes a subsidy tailwind rather than disrupting Hexatronic's core.
  • Positive disruption. The "copper wall" in AI clusters forces optical links closer to the chip; leaf-spine architectures push fibre counts per connection from 2 to 12-16 cores; data-centre optical-cable demand is expected to grow about 20% a year to 2031.
  • Adverse, slower forces. Multicore fibre (Corning, Sumitomo, AFL and TeraHop completed a four-core standard in September 2026), hollow-core fibre (about 30% lower latency, supply-limited, more likely after 2028) and co-packaged optics move value to component and standards owners. Hyperscalers increasingly buy glass direct and secure dark fibre (Verizon's USD 1bn-plus agreement with Google), bypassing intermediaries. Digital twins and AI-assisted planning could lower the labour content of installation, the basis of Hexatronic's system-selling advantage, over three years.
  • Direction on the company. Positive for volumes through about 2028; neutral-to-negative for value capture beyond it. Hexatronic participates as a volume seller, not as a technology setter.
C-04

To what extent is political or regulatory change under way, and how does it bear on the company?

C58

Judgement. Political and regulatory change is a modest net tailwind whose earnings effect lies mostly in 2027 and later; the near-term changes (BEAD made technology-neutral and delayed, permitting bottlenecks, Hexatronic's own cable line not yet BABA-compliant) mean regulation does not justify any uplift to the next 12 months' forecasts, while defence spending and US localisation rules support the mid-term.

  • BEAD (USD 42.45bn). Restructured in June 2025 (fibre-first removed). State subgrantee contracts are now being signed (Washington's first seven in September 2026; Indiana's USD 868m allocation in October 2026) with four-year construction deadlines; most hardware orders become vendor revenue in 2027. A study identified 86,402 potential permitting events across 6,933 project areas, which throttles early build speed. Early-adopter states allocated 80-95% to fibre. Management treats BEAD as upside to private builds, which is the right framing for the model.
  • BABA and tariffs. Hexatronic makes all its US ducts and microducts locally, so it qualifies for subsidised duct demand, and certified domestic makers command a 10-20% premium (Calix regional vice president, July 2025). Its fibre-cable line move to South Carolina was postponed to 2027, so its cables are not yet BABA-compliant for subsidised cable contracts.
  • Defence and security. European defence budgets support Harsh Environment connectivity (record Q3 adjusted EBITA of SEK 43m); the JOWO acquisition needed German Foreign Trade and Payments Act clearance, which shows defence-adjacent deals carry approval delay. Submarine-cable security and route diversity support the subsea business.
  • Europe. Lighter-touch access regulation and gigabit subsidies exist but have not revived FTTH demand. EU procurement localisation (Net-Zero Industry Act criteria) could favour local makers but is unquantified.
  • Site-level risk. The Hudiksvall environmental permit caps production volume; the company met all conditions except the volume cap, and a revised permit was expected during 2026. Hudiksvall is where the subsea capacity doubling (operational 2028) will sit, so permit delay would push the 2028 step.
C-05

How are input costs, input availability and supplier concentration changing, and is the net effect a headwind or a tailwind?

❌ D35

Judgement. Input conditions are a clear and worsening headwind, but the binding effect has shifted from margin to availability: glass fibre and selected connectors now restrict Hexatronic's growth and lengthen lead times, while Q3 2026 gross margin actually rose, so pass-through is working for now. The structural problem is that a non-integrated assembler is a price-taker at the bottleneck; the structural relief arrives only with new preform capacity around 2028.

  • Prices. Standard G.652.D fibre spot rose from RMB 18 to RMB 90-100 per fibre-km between mid-2025 and mid-2026 (Jefferies); since July 2025 prices rose 5.5x in China, 4x in India, 3.6x in Europe and 1.6x in the US. Raw-material expenses were SEK 1,377m in Q2 2026 against SEK 1,141m a year earlier (+21% against sales +18%). Transport costs rose in Q3; resin has stabilised at lower levels.
  • Availability. TrendForce estimates a 180m fibre-km shortfall in 2026 at 92% utilisation, with shortages possibly lasting through 2028. Management disclosed on 22 October 2026 that glass fibre and selected connectors "were beginning to restrict growth", with rising backlogs and unavoidable lead-time extensions, and that it is validating new suppliers.
  • Concentration. The top five preform makers hold about 67% of capacity (Corning about 20%); only ten to twelve companies draw glass; Corning is at once a supplier and a competitor; Meta's up-to-USD 6bn order locks Corning capacity for years; vertically integrated makers are protected while non-integrated cable makers in Europe and India are exposed (Jefferies). Preform greenfield takes up to four years.
  • Margin evidence so far. Adjusted gross margin rose to 39.8% in Q3 2026 from 37.5% in Q3 2025 (after falling to 38.7% from 40.1% in Q2). Index-linked contracts with about a three-month lag, US price rises "with the industry" and mix toward Data Center explain it. CFO guidance in July of "temporary pressure on margins in the third quarter" did not materialise at the group level.
  • Net effect. Headwind via (1) capped volume upside in a strong demand year, (2) inventory and working-capital build, and (3) margin risk if the index lag lengthens or if a supplier allocates away. Possible offset: scarcity lets Hexatronic prioritise higher-margin data-centre, subsea and defence orders.
  • Implication for the model. Cap 2027 volume growth below demand; hold gross margin near Q2-Q3 2026 levels rather than expanding it; add working capital per unit of growth; treat post-2028 input prices as a potential tailwind when new capacity lands.
C-06

Where is the company's own revenue and demand cycle, and to what extent is it turning?

B75

Judgement. The company's own revenue and demand cycle has turned decisively up and was still accelerating in Q3 2026, but it is now at or near the peak rate of change: the next two quarters bring seasonal softness and Data Center normalisation, and from Q2 2027 the comparisons become hard. The question for the model is the level the business settles at, not the direction.

  • Turn evidence. Group organic: 2025 +3% (Fiber Solutions -5%), Q1 2026 -2%, Q2 +11%, Q3 +17%. Adjusted EBITA margin: trough 7.2% (Q4 2025), then 8.6%, 10.0%, 11.3%. Fiber Solutions margin: 5.2% (Q4 2025) to 9.1% (Q2) to 9.4% (Q3). Order book about four months of sales at Q3, unchanged from Q2, up from about 2.5 months at Q1.
  • Where each part sits. US Fiber Solutions: early-to-mid upswing. Data Center: mid-boom, with Q3 a local peak. Fiber Solutions Europe: trough, flat-to-down. Harsh Environment: defence up, energy down.
  • Against the last cycle. FY2026 revenue (roughly SEK 8.5-8.7bn on Q4 seasonality) will exceed the 2023 peak of SEK 8.15bn in reported terms, about half of the gain acquired; margins (about 10%) remain well below the 2022-23 peak (15-16.6%), because European ducts no longer earn boom margins and services carry a lower gross margin.
  • Turning risk. The second derivative turns negative in H1 2027 as Data Center normalises and the US comparisons harden. A first-derivative turn (absolute decline) needs either a US refill ending abruptly or a hyperscaler pause; neither is visible in Q3 data.
C-07

To what extent is a macro force acting on the company, on its sector and on its industry, and at which level does it bite hardest?

❌ D40

Judgement. Macro forces act on Hexatronic mainly through two channels at company level, currency translation and the discount rate applied to a small-cap cyclical, and through one channel at sector level, AI-capex sentiment; the macro effect on underlying demand is mixed and less important than the stock-level effects. Macro bites hardest at the company level.

  • Rates. US private builders are rate-sensitive (they paused in 2025 and restarted as rates fell). The regime has since turned into a discount-rate shock: US 10-year about 5.1%, hikes pulled forward, the market "refusing leverage, duration and projects" (Fitness for Fighting, 16 October 2026). A renewed rate rise would slow marginal alt-net and overbuild projects (a former Charter executive says business cases were built on lower rates and broadband access spend peaks around 2028).
  • Currency. More than 90% of sales are outside Sweden; translation cut reported sales 5% in 2025 and 9 points in Q1 2026, added 1% in Q3 2026. A Riksbank hike is "increasingly likely" in November 2026 (Barclays), implying a stronger krona and a renewed translation drag. Operating profit is largely protected by local production (10% krona strengthening: GBP -SEK 21m, USD -SEK 8m, EUR +SEK 7m). No financial transaction hedging; net-investment hedging via foreign-currency borrowing.
  • AI-capex financing. The first credit crack inside the AI build (CoreWeave 2032 bonds below 78 cents) hits Data Center sentiment first and demand only at second order. The optics and fibre cohort fell 4.5% over three months on "AI monetization anxiety" while Hexatronic was +118% over twelve months.
  • Europe. Weak German construction, contractor labour shortages (30% of German contractors), UK cost-of-living pressure; they prolong FTTH weakness rather than create new damage.
  • Level. Company level (translation, small-cap fragility rated Double Underweight, no anchor owner) is where macro bites hardest; sector level (AI optics) second; industry level (Technology neutral) least.
  • Implication for the model. Carry an explicit krona scenario: a 5-10% stronger krona removes 4-9% from reported revenue with a smaller proportional effect on EBITA.
C-08

To what extent is a key driver at the industry or sector level accelerating the company's demand?

C72

Judgement. Yes: the AI data-centre build is directly and materially accelerating Hexatronic's demand, both in Data Center and, less visibly, in Fiber Solutions through transport and middle-mile links; defence readiness is a second accelerating driver in Harsh Environment. Together they now touch roughly 40-45% of sales, against about 27% in 2024. The acceleration is real but capped by glass supply and exposed to a financing-led pause.

  • Data centre. Organic +44% in Q3 2026; sales above SEK 690m a quarter (from below SEK 500m a year earlier); management sees no slowing in underlying momentum and expects cloud to outgrow enterprise. External: data-centre capacity 97 GW (2024) to 226 GW (2030); a Meta procurement manager cites planning horizons up to five years and subsea demand growth of 25% a year in North America.
  • Transport and middle-mile. Management cites rising US transport-network demand linked to data-centre interconnection; Viper Core transport-grade cable is the product; the sell-side calls this underestimated. This is how the AI theme reaches the "legacy" segment.
  • Defence. Drone fibre consumption rose from 1% to 15% of global demand; European budgets rising; Harsh Environment connectivity has a strong order book.
  • Limits. Hexatronic is a volume seller, not a scarcity seller (Sector Digest, 13 October 2026): it does not own the constrained glass, so its acceleration is limited by allocation, and it is more exposed to the build itself than to the shortage window.
C-09

What other material external dynamic, positive or negative, is at work?

C55

Judgement. Four other external dynamics are material: the supply shortage as an allocation lever (potentially positive), consolidation of the installer market by private equity and strategic buyers (negative for acquisition returns), labour scarcity in network construction (two-sided), and subsea infrastructure security (positive). Net, mildly positive.

  • Allocation under scarcity. When supply binds, Hexatronic can prioritise data-centre, subsea and defence orders where buyers prioritise availability over price; this could lift mix and margin even as volume growth is capped. Unproven: no realised price or lost-tender data disclosed.
  • Installer consolidation. Private-equity buy-and-build platforms (Wize Solutions) are expanding coverage and bidding for targets; Redeye notes larger data-centre targets attract higher private-equity valuations. "Strategic buyers set the benchmark for installer networks" (Sector Digest). This threatens the 5-7x EBITDA entry multiples on which Hexatronic's acquisition-led targets depend.
  • Labour scarcity. Installation labour, not materials, is the deployment bottleneck in fibre (expert calls); this favours Hexatronic's labour-saving blown-fibre systems, but raises the cost and caps the capacity of its own data-centre service business (wage growth not expected below 4%; trained technicians scarce).
  • Subsea resilience. Sabotage risk and route diversity raise demand for armoured, buried, sensed submarine cable; the NKT framework (to 2032) is the vehicle.
C-10

Taking each sector and industry thematic driver in turn, and then all of the above together: how strong are the external headwinds and how strong are the tailwinds at value-chain, industry and sector level, and what is the overall external change picture?

C66

Judgement, driver by driver, then overall. The overall external picture is the most favourable since 2022, with tailwinds clearly outweighing headwinds for the next 6-12 months, but the quality of the tailwind is cyclical and thematic rather than contracted, the rate of change has probably peaked, and the configuration is asymmetric: supply constraints cap the upside while a US refill ending or an AI-capex pause would open the downside.

  • AI data-centre optics (sector and industry level): strong tailwind. Structural, multi-year; Hexatronic participates as installer and volume supplier; financing scrutiny rising.
  • US broadband rebuild, private and BEAD (value-chain level): moderate-to-strong tailwind. Cyclical with a subsidised tail to 2030; partly refill; BEAD revenue mostly 2027+.
  • European FTTH maturity (value-chain level): structural headwind. Fiber Solutions Europe falling 9-31% a quarter in 2026; no recovery before 2028; it needs only to stop falling for the thesis.
  • Defence readiness (sector level): tailwind. Long window (to 2035), timing lumpy.
  • Glass and component shortage (value-chain level): headwind for a non-integrated assembler on availability and bargaining power; potential post-2028 tailwind when capacity lands.
  • Competition (value-chain level): benign now in the US and data centres, hostile in Europe, tightening from 2028.
  • Macro (company level): headwind through the krona and the discount rate.
  • Regulation: small tailwind, back-ended.
  • Overall. Net external force: positive and strong for 2026-27, neutral to negative for 2028-29 as capacity arrives. The model should front-load growth in 2026-27 and taper it, and should treat the krona and the end of US refill as the two swing variables for 2027 reported revenue.

Internal change forces

C6213 questions
C-1148C-1268C-1342C-1462C-1542C-1662C-1752C-1866C-1935C-2063C-8638C-8755C-2164

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The internal story is a genuine, multi-dimensional corporate change led by an external chief executive, and it has travelled the whole chain from resources to choices to actions to operating metrics to the accounts within eighteen months: the cost programme over-delivered, the mix moved toward higher-margin niches, three cheap acquisitions were made and are performing, the balance sheet was repaired with equity, and disclosure improved. This is the part of the case that would survive a pause in the external cycle, because the lower cost base and the mix are the company's own doing. The weaknesses are concentrated rather than diffuse: management depth has thinned at the growth engine and in finance just as complexity peaks; the change has no capital-return dimension; and the targets are destination-only, with the incentive plan's 9.8-11.1% average margin range quietly indicating the board's realistic expectation below the 12% headline. For the model, the corporate change supports a structurally higher margin floor in Fiber Solutions (around 8-9% even on flat volumes, against 5-6% in 2025) and continued acquired growth of roughly 3-6% of sales a year, but it does not support further self-help margin gains beyond volume and mix, and it argues for no dividend and modest dilution.

Horizon: the last 6 months and the next 6 months, compared with the last 12 to 18 months.

C-11

To what extent have the chief executive, or other key leaders (e.g. finance director, operations director, board, division heads, etc.) or other key actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) changed, and what does the change signal?

❌ D48

Judgement. Leadership has changed substantially in two waves. The first (March 2025: an external chief executive, Rikard Fröberg) was the trigger for everything positive since. The second (June to October 2026) is a reshaping of the team around three segment presidents, but it removes the deputy chief executive who built the acquisition programme, leaves the fastest-growing segment under an acting head, and opens a CFO vacancy precisely when acquisitions, contingent consideration and supply management raise financial complexity. It signals a chief executive building his own operating model with clearer accountability, at the cost of thinner execution capacity for the next two to four quarters; there is no evidence of performance-driven exits.

  • Chief executive. Fröberg, from 1 March 2025: McKinsey, Ahlsell (over 20 acquisitions; Ahlsell is a Hexatronic customer), 15 years at Ansell ending as President of a USD 800m global business unit. No fibre background and no prior listed-company chief-executive role. He launched the performance improvement programme four months into the role.
  • Second wave. Deputy CEO Martin Åberg (in group management since 2014; M&A and Data Center) resigned in June 2026 to focus on private investments, with a nine-month notice period. Oscar Wärme (Head of M&A since 2022) is acting Head of Data Center from 1 August 2026; no permanent appointment by the Q3 report. CFO Pernilla Lindén (since 2021, ex-Handicare) becomes President Fiber Solutions on 1 November 2026, staying CFO until an externally recruited successor arrives; none named. Magnus Angermund leaves the executive team but stays Head of Fiber Solutions Europe. Christian Priess (EMEA) left in February 2026. A Head of Investor Relations left after one year (replaced January 2026). Jakob Skov (Harsh Environment) continues.
  • Board and owners. Chair Magnus Nicolin since 2024; four of six directors appointed in 2024 (average tenure 2.6 years); Accendo Capital (about 5%) on the nomination committee.
  • What it signals. Positive reading: putting the architect of the Fiber Solutions cost programme in charge of the largest segment strengthens accountability for the margin recovery, and three presidents on the executive team align decision rights with segment targets. Negative reading: the M&A engine loses its architect while the plan needs three to six deals a year; a finance-control gap opens with earn-outs (JOWO up to EUR 7.6m, Superior up to USD 3m), a SEK 2.09bn liquidity pool and a 2028 subsea investment; Data Center, which delivered about three quarters of Q3's profit surprise, has no permanent leader.
  • Watch. Permanent CFO and Data Center appointments by the Q4 2026 report (February 2027); if not, the risk becomes a cockroach ingredient alongside Harsh Environment and supply.
C-12

To what extent have the strategy or the strategic priorities changed, and how well is the change articulated and resourced? (choices)

C68

Judgement. The strategy changed materially in September 2025 (from an FTTH volume business to a three-segment portfolio pivoting to data centres and harsh environments, with 2028 segment targets) and has been re-tuned in the last six months from defence to offence: Fiber Solutions moved from restructuring to growth, the acquisition hierarchy became explicit, Data Center services broadened, and subsea became an internal growth platform through the NKT partnership. The destination is well articulated and financially resourced; the bridge (organic versus acquired growth, acquisition budget, return hurdles, EPS path) is not articulated, and the human resourcing is thinner than the financial resourcing.

  • The pivot is visible in the mix. Data Center plus Harsh Environment rose from 27% of sales (2024) to 37% (end-2025) to 44% (Q3 2026), and from about 56% of adjusted EBITA (2025) to about 55% (Q3 2026, because Fiber Solutions margins recovered).
  • Recent changes. Clinton downsizing reversed after North American sales grew 80%; M&A prospecting prioritised to Data Center and Harsh Environment, opportunistic in Fiber Solutions; Data Center services extended to design, project management, migration, security, audio-visual, access and campus solutions; a US multi-hub Data Center footprint (Communication Zone in Chicago, Superior in Texas, existing Dallas); the NKT framework (to 2032) more than doubling Hudiksvall subsea capacity from about SEK 150-200m to above SEK 500m a year from 2028.
  • Resourcing. SEK 600m equity raised in May 2026; adjusted leverage 1.3x; available liquidity SEK 2,090m. People: the bench is thinner (C-11), and a services-heavy strategy needs scarce technicians.
  • Articulation gaps. No acquisition budget, no minimum return threshold, no accretion target, no organic/acquired split of the SEK 11bn 2028 target; Redeye's model excludes future acquisitions while management's target includes them.
  • Core versus adjacency (Wisdom Library). Data-centre cabling and installation share customers and skills with the core; security, audio-visual and campus solutions are further out on the adjacency ring, where distant moves fail about three times in four. The small size and low entry multiples of the deals limit the cost of being wrong.
C-13

To what extent has financial guidance been raised, or have mid-term targets or capital-markets-day ambitions been set or raised? (choices)

❌ D42

Judgement. Guidance has not been raised. The company gives no numeric annual guidance, set its 2028 targets in September 2025 and May 2026 (group SEK 11bn sales, EBITA margin about 12%; Fiber Solutions SEK 6bn at 10%, Harsh Environment SEK 2bn at 15%, Data Center SEK 3bn at 15%, acquisitions included) and has maintained them through three beats, including the large Q3 2026 beat. The most informative signal is the incentive plan: LTIP 2026 vests on an average EBITA margin of 9.8% to 11.1% over 2026-28, which implies the board's own planning range for the next three years sits around 10-11%, below the 12% headline target.

  • Near-term qualitative guidance is cautious. For Q4 2026: Data Center normalises toward the first-half pattern, Q4 and Q1 are seasonally lower than Q2-Q3, submarine shipments similar to Q3, continued US growth, Europe subdued. In July 2026 the CFO warned of temporary margin pressure in Q3 from raw materials; the group margin instead rose to 11.3%.
  • What the targets imply. From roughly SEK 8.6bn in 2026 to SEK 11bn in 2028 is about 13% a year including acquisitions. On segment run-rates the Data Center target looks conservative (FY2026 about SEK 2.2bn, so about 17% a year to SEK 3bn), Fiber Solutions moderately demanding (about SEK 4.95bn to SEK 6bn, about 10% a year, needing continued US growth and a stable Europe), Harsh Environment stretched (about SEK 1.4bn to SEK 2bn at a 15% margin, needing acquisitions and a margin step from 11%).
  • No capital-markets-day ambition raised. None scheduled in the files.
C-14

To what extent are standards improving in execution, operational measures, governance or cost discipline? (choices and actions)

C62

Judgement. Standards have improved clearly in cost discipline, operating measures and disclosure, and the improvement is visible in the accounts; the laggards are working capital, capacity-commissioning discipline and governance scaffolding (no internal audit function).

  • Cost and operations. Performance improvement programme delivered SEK 120m of annual savings against a SEK 110m target, ahead of schedule (finalised Q1 2026): Weterings (Netherlands) closed with 120 roles removed, Clinton downsized (SEK 10m, now reversed), European duct production consolidated. Fiber Solutions adjusted EBITA margin 5.4% (Q3 2025) to 9.4% (Q3 2026), three consecutive sequential improvements. Adjusted operating costs fell to 25.0% of sales from 26.2% (Q3 year on year).
  • Disclosure. Voluntary three-segment reporting with organic growth, margin and geography each quarter; a company-commissioned consensus before each print; explicit Q4 normalisation warning.
  • Incentives. LTIP 2026 weights diluted EPS 40%, sales growth 25%, EBITA margin 25% and sustainability 10%, measured on a three-year average (rebased after consultation with institutional holders), which removes the incentive to time one year.
  • Laggards. Net working capital 26.1% of trailing sales at Q2 2026; nine-month 2026 working capital outflow SEK 205m; DSO 56-60 days; Q3 cash conversion 72%. The capacity record shows repeated slips (Clinton start-up delayed two months in 2022; South Carolina full operation deferred to late Q3 2023; Ogden postponed from Q2 to Q3 2024; South Carolina cable lines deferred to 2027). Governance: PwC sole auditor since 2013, no internal audit function, thin director shareholdings.
C-15

To what extent is the company reducing its perimeter or composition, through divestments, simplification or exits from low-return units? (actions)

❌ D42

Judgement. The company is not reducing its perimeter in any meaningful sense; it is reducing its European factory footprint while expanding its perimeter by acquisition. There have been no divestments or exits from low-return units, and the one perimeter cut in the US (Clinton) has been reversed. For predictability this is a mild negative: the number of operating units grows by three to six a year by policy.

  • Footprint reduction: Weterings closure (2025); European microduct consolidation; Fiber Solutions simplified into four commercial regions plus global product and sourcing functions; executive team cut from eight to seven.
  • Perimeter expansion: Communication Zone (November 2025), JOWO (April 2026), Superior (June 2026), each kept as an independent legal entity; employees 2,011 (end-2025) to 2,133 (Q3 2026), mainly acquired.
  • No disposals. No sale of a core or non-core business; European duct capacity, whose returns are lowest, is retained (and arguably needed for Europe's shift to connection products).
C-16

To what extent is the company entering new geographic or product markets, and how sensibly? (actions)

C62

Judgement. Entry into new markets is active and mostly sensible because it is adjacent, small-ticket and cheaply acquired: new US data-centre hubs and end-markets, German defence connectors, transport-grade products and Asia-Pacific growth. The less sensible edge is the broadening of Data Center services into security, audio-visual and access solutions, which are further from the core capabilities and more labour-dependent.

  • Geography. US data-centre hubs (Chicago via Communication Zone, Texas via Superior, Dallas), the western US (Ogden duct plant), Germany (JOWO), Asia-Pacific sales +37% in Q3 2026 on Fiber Solutions and Harsh Environment, with management citing emerging-market opportunities.
  • Products. Viper Core transport cable (middle-mile, blowing distances above 3,000m); VIRIDIS jacketed wireline (doubling dynamic-cable life); Data Center services; subsea capacity expansion (operational 2028).
  • End-markets. Superior extends Data Center beyond hyperscalers into education, healthcare, government and commercial customers, which diversifies concentration but at a lower margin (Superior's EBITDA margin about 12.5% against the segment's 15-17%).
  • Sensibility test. Deals under EUR 50m at 5-7x EBITDA, founder-led, with earn-outs; the sector evidence is that such bolt-ons create value while larger growth-led deals produce write-downs. The risk is managerial bandwidth, not capital.
C-17

To what extent is the company raising prices in real terms (above cost inflation), and are the rises real, merely nominal, or so far only latent or plausible and increasingly noted by management? (actions)

C52

Judgement. Price rises are real in nominal terms and so far sufficient to protect gross margin, but they are cost-driven pass-through, not demonstrated real price increases; real pricing above cost inflation is latent and plausible only in the scarce segments (data centre, subsea, defence) where buyers prioritise availability over price, and management is increasingly vocal about pricing.

  • Evidence of application. US price rises passed through in 2026 "backed by industry-wide pricing hikes"; Q3 2026 management: "increasing prices to offset higher fibre and transportation costs"; CEO (April 2026): "we are responding with price increases"; CEO argues passive materials are less than 20% of a fibre deployment's cost, so customers absorb increases. Adjusted gross margin rose 230bp year on year in Q3 2026 (39.8%), helped by volume absorption and mix as well as price.
  • Against. Europe: overcapacity, price falls with volume. 2024-25: US duct pricing was lower year on year, offset by volume. Management withholds the magnitude of increases "for competitive reasons". Redeye's assessment: a fragmented market with commodity-like products and limited ability to raise prices above inflation without churn.
  • Latent real pricing. Hyperscalers and military buyers prioritise supply over price (Jefferies, October 2026); scarce, BABA-compliant local capacity can command a 10-20% premium in subsidised builds. This is where real pricing could appear in 2027, and it is unproven.
  • Classification: nominal, with latent and increasingly noted real-pricing potential in scarce segments.
C-18

To what extent is the company stepping up mergers and acquisitions, and how strategically sensible, disciplined and well integrated are they? (actions)

C66

Judgement. Acquisitions have stepped up sharply and so far look strategically sensible, disciplined on price and well integrated, with early outperformance; the risks are that the M&A architect has left, that rising private-equity competition will lift entry multiples, that service acquisitions dilute group gross margin and depend on scarce labour, and that success itself raises earn-out liabilities that hit reported earnings.

  • Deals. Communication Zone (US data-centre services, Chicago, November 2025); JOWO Systemtechnik (German defence connectors, EUR 11.8m EV, 4.7x EV/EBITDA, April 2026; maximum additional consideration EUR 7.6m on 2026-2030 earnings); Superior Fiber & Data Services (Texas installation, USD 29m, 5.8-6.4x, effectively 5.4-6.0x after a tax reorganisation; about USD 40m revenue and USD 5m normalised EBITDA; maximum additional USD 3m on 2026-29 earnings). The 2026 acquisitions cost an estimated SEK 551m including contingent consideration and contributed SEK 272m of sales from closing to Q3; acquired growth was 14 points of Q3's 33%.
  • Discipline and integration. Framework: founder-led targets under EUR 50m at 5-7x EBITDA, kept as independent entities with local profit responsibility; Communication Zone delivered a material project for a large customer and Superior strengthened its education position, both ahead of Redeye's expectations in Q3; Data Center margin held at 16.9% despite Superior's lower margin.
  • Implicit return. At 5-7x EBITDA, pre-synergy post-tax returns on capital are roughly 11-15%, above the cost of capital but not high; the value creation depends on keeping multiples low.
  • Risks. Åberg's departure; a stated cadence of three to six deals a year; private-equity platforms bidding up larger targets; contingent-consideration revaluations (SEK 28m charge in Q3 2026 net financial items); decentralised integration limits visible synergies; the risk report's "false friend" reading (acquired growth masking core decay) is weakened by Q3's 17% organic growth but not removed.
C-19

To what extent is the company making more use of its balance sheet (for investment, acquisitions or returns), or stepping up shareholder returns (a buyback starting or increasing, a special or progressive dividend), or is management increasingly mooting either? (actions)

❌ D35

Judgement. The balance sheet is being used for growth, not for shareholder returns, and it was rebuilt with equity rather than debt; there is no dividend, no buyback and no sign that management is mooting either. With adjusted leverage at 1.3x and SEK 2.09bn of liquidity, capacity for further acquisitions is ample, so any capital-return step would be a surprise.

  • Balance sheet. Net debt excluding leases SEK 1,305m at Q3 2026 (from SEK 3,094m at September 2023 and SEK 1,582m at end-2025); adjusted leverage 1.3x (2.2x Q1, 1.7x Q2); covenants on leverage excluding IFRS 16, with ample headroom.
  • Equity. SEK 600m directed issue in May 2026 at SEK 38 (8.2% discount, 15,789,474 new shares, about 7.7% of the prior share count), oversubscribed, no rights offer; a 90-day lock-up for board and management followed.
  • Returns. Dividend per share zero for FY2023-25 and zero in consensus to FY2028; stated policy is acquisitions over dividends; buybacks only via incentive-programme hedging; LTIP dilution about 0.41% for the new programme and 2.18% including existing rights.
  • Implication for the model. No dividend before 2028; share count drifting up by roughly 0.5-1% a year from incentives, with a further directed issue possible if a larger acquisition appears.
C-20

What other material positive corporate change forces are present/plausible?

C63

Judgement. The other material positive corporate changes are the NKT subsea partnership (a contracted capacity step for 2028), the Clinton reversal (management committing capacity to demonstrated US demand), the product cadence into transport and data centres, and the coming CFO refresh, which could tighten capital discipline. They are real but individually second-order to the cost programme and the mix shift.

  • NKT partnership (29 June 2026): a new Hudiksvall line, operational in 2028, raising subsea capacity from about SEK 150-200m to above SEK 500m a year, with a significant portion reserved for NKT through 2032. Redeye estimates about SEK 300m of investment and about SEK 325m of eventual sales at a 20% EBITA margin; Hexatronic has not disclosed the investment amount.
  • Clinton reversal: retains US capacity into rising demand and supports operating leverage, at the cost of fixed costs re-entering.
  • Products: Viper Core transport cable; customer-connection products for Europe's shift from passing to connecting (Hexatronic UK's filing notes operators moving purchases from cables to connection products).
  • CFO refresh: an external CFO arriving during a period of acquisitions can act as a capital-discipline reset (Wisdom Library pattern), though it is a transition risk until done.
  • Incentive rebasing to a three-year average after holder consultation.
C-86

What negative corporate change developments are present/plausible?

❌ D38

Judgement. The negative corporate developments present or plausible are a cluster rather than one large problem: leadership thinning at the growth engine, supply constraints converting into working-capital build and capped growth, timing-flattered Data Center results that will normalise, a Harsh Environment margin stuck well below target, equity-funded acquisitions with rising contingent liabilities, and capacity decisions that swing with the cycle. Three or more at once fits the framework's cockroach pattern, which is the main reason to discount the Q3 beat rather than to dismiss it.

  • Present: deputy CEO departure; acting Data Center head; CFO search; Q4 Data Center normalisation flagged by management; Harsh Environment organic growth only 6% in Q3 (-4% in Q2), dynamic cables hit by deferred oil-and-gas orders, margin 11.0% against a 15% target; glass and connector constraints; nine-month working capital outflow SEK 205m and Q3 cash conversion 72%; SEK 28m of contingent-consideration revaluation in Q3 net financial items (negative SEK 73m in total).
  • Plausible: further directed share issues to fund acquisitions; a goodwill impairment if an acquired service business underperforms; a delayed or restrictive Hudiksvall permit pushing the 2028 subsea step; insider selling after the lock-up and the deputy CEO's notice period; renewed capacity cuts if US demand turns (the downsize-then-reverse pattern indicates planning volatility); margin squeeze if pass-through lags a further glass price step.
C-87

To what extent have key controlling actors (e.g. activists, major shareholders) indicated scope for change in strategy/approach?

C55

Judgement. No controlling actor exists and none has signalled a change of strategy: the largest holder controls only 9.7% of votes, and the most active shareholder, Accendo Capital (about 5%, nomination-committee representative), backs the pivot and balance-sheet discipline. The evidence of holder influence is on governance and incentives, not on strategy; the latent scope for change is a future push for capital returns once leverage is low, which no holder has yet made.

  • Accendo Capital: 5.16% in April 2026 (about 4.69% cited elsewhere), Mark Shay on the nomination committee for the 2026 and 2027 AGMs; proposed Nicolin as chair; described as prioritising balance-sheet strength and self-funded acquisitions over dividends. The chair holds 200,000 call options issued by Accendo, which aligns him with that holder.
  • Institutions: fully subscribed the SEK 600m issue; LTIP 2026 rebased to a three-year average after consultation with institutional holders.
  • Absence of an anchor owner cuts both ways: no blocking power against a hostile approach, and potentially slower decisions in a downturn.
C-21

Taken together, how strong, broad and well-resourced are the positive corporate changes under way?

C64

Judgement. Taken together, the positive corporate changes are strong, broad and financially well resourced, and unusually for a turnaround they have already shown in the profit-and-loss account: cost (SEK 120m), structure (three segments), portfolio mix (44% of sales in the growth segments), acquisitions (three, cheap), capacity (subsea, Clinton) and disclosure. What keeps them short of top-tier is that the human resourcing has thinned at the moment of scaling, that no capital-return lever is in play, and that the strategic bridge from today's business to the 2028 targets is unquantified.

  • Strength: proven in outputs (Fiber Solutions margin up 400bp year on year; group margin 11.3%).
  • Breadth: four of twelve internal forces strong (chief executive, strategy, standards, acquisitions), four moderate, two weak (guidance, shareholder returns).
  • Resourcing: money yes (SEK 2.09bn liquidity, 1.3x leverage); people stretched (C-11).
  • Durability: the cost programme is complete rather than continuing, so further margin progress must come from volume, mix and pricing, not from more self-help.

Industry and Value Chain

❌ D4216 questions
C-2435C-2530C-2642C-2745C-2845C-2938C-3140C-8455C-3262C-3360C-3442C-3545C-3665C-3756C-3845C-3930

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

Hexatronic sits in the wrong layer of an attractive value chain: demand for fibre is growing strongly in data centres and recovering in the US, but the profit pool and the pricing power sit upstream with five glass makers (about 67% of preform capacity), one of which is both Hexatronic's main supplier and a strengthening competitor, while Hexatronic's own layer (ducts, cable assembly, installation) is fragmented, easy to enter in services, and in Europe structurally oversupplied with competitors who cut price when volume falls. Today's supportive conditions (industry price rises in the US, hyperscalers prioritising availability, BABA protection) are borrowed from a temporary scarcity and a cyclical upswing and carry an approximate expiry around 2027-28, when the integrated majors' capacity arrives. Hexatronic's genuine structural protections are narrow: blown-fibre system selling with installer lock-in, local compliant capacity in the US, defence certifications and the NKT subsea framework. For the model, this means industry structure supports margins and pricing through 2027 but should be assumed to revert thereafter, European pricing should be modelled as volume-linked, and data-centre growth should be credited mainly to installation and services rather than to scarce hardware.

C-24

Where does the company rank by market share or size for its activity or product, and how far is it from the number one or two position?

❌ D35

Judgement. Hexatronic is not the number one or two in any large market it serves; it is a niche leader-candidate in blown-fibre microduct systems for small and mid-sized operators (Nordics, UK, Germany, New Zealand, US tier-2/3 builders) and a small player in everything adjacent. Its distance from the leaders is very large in optical fibre and cable (it does not make glass) and large in data-centre connectivity; it is closest to leadership in its blown-fibre system niche and in selected harsh-environment niches, where no source ranks it.

  • Scale against leaders. Revenue about SEK 8.6bn in 2026 (about EUR 0.75bn) against Prysmian EUR 19.7bn (2025), Corning's Optical Communications about USD 2.07bn a quarter, Nexans about EUR 7.8bn, NKT EUR 2.7bn; about 1% US market share; 2,133 employees against Prysmian's 33,000.
  • Positioning by segment. Fiber Solutions: a targeted niche specialist with Emtelle and Dura-Line in blown fibre, microducts and conduits; competes head-on with Nexans in Scandinavia; Dura-Line targets tier-1 operators, Hexatronic PE-backed emerging carriers. Data Center: one of many cabling and installation providers against Corning, Amphenol (with CommScope's connectivity business), Fujikura, Lightera and large contractors. Harsh Environment: specialist connectors and dynamic cables against Amphenol, TE Connectivity, Radiall and others.
  • Implication. Hexatronic cannot set prices in its main markets; its returns depend on niche differentiation and cycle position, not on market leadership.
C-25

How concentrated is the market: do the top five hold more than 30 percent, and how steep is the share distribution (for example, do the top five hold five times the share of numbers six to ten)?

❌ D30

Judgement. The market is two-tier: highly concentrated upstream, where the profit pool sits, and fragmented downstream, where Hexatronic operates. Upstream, the top five preform makers hold about 67% of capacity with a steep curve; downstream ducts, cable assembly and installation have no published share data and are described as fragmented with overcapacity, so the top-five-above-30% and five-times tests are met only in the layer that Hexatronic buys from, not in the layer it sells into.

  • Upstream (glass preform): Corning about 20%, YOFC 15%, ZTT 11%, Furukawa 8%, Prysmian about 5% (top five about 67%); Corning holds about 50-65% of the US optical-fibre market; only ten to twelve companies globally draw glass. Steepness above five times the next five is plausible but not evidenced.
  • Downstream: microducts and ducts fragmented in Europe (many regional extruders, Chinese imports); installation services fragmented with low entry barriers; US building wire (not relevant to Hexatronic) is a duopoly.
  • Consequence. Value accrues upstream (preform is about 80% of bare-fibre manufacturing cost; glass prices up multiples since mid-2025) while Hexatronic's layer is competitive, which caps its structural margin.
C-26

How stable are market shares over time, and what share have new entrants taken over the last three to five years?

❌ D42

Judgement. Market shares are not stable in Hexatronic's layer: they move with regional cycles and price behaviour. Hexatronic gained share in the US through the downturn and lost volume with the European market; new entrants have taken little durable share in cable and ducts, but entry is easy in installation services, where private-equity roll-ups are gaining share.

  • US: Hexatronic grew while larger domestic peers reported double-digit organic declines in 2024-25 (business-foundations research); North American sales +80% in Q3 2026 against Dura-Line +23% (Q1 2026), consistent with share gains, though no share data are disclosed.
  • Europe: Fiber Solutions Europe -31% / -10% / -9% in Q1-Q3 2026 in a shrinking market; share not measurable; Chinese exporters took price rather than durable share.
  • New entrants (last three to five years): in data-centre cabling, Tier-2 suppliers approved by hyperscalers (OCC at Meta) and fast customisers (viaPhoton) win selected projects; in installation, PE buy-and-build platforms (Wize Solutions) are consolidating; in submarine and high-voltage, Hellenic Cables, LS Cable, Taihan and Sumitomo are adding capacity, mainly in power cable rather than in Hexatronic's fibre subsea niche.
C-27

To what extent does market share consolidate over time towards the largest competitors, and towards the most advantaged ones (not necessarily the largest or most established), and is this company among them?

❌ D45

Judgement. Share consolidates toward the largest competitors at the glass and data-centre-hardware end, and toward the most advantaged (local, BABA-compliant capacity, short lead times, installation capability) in US ducts and system selling. Hexatronic is among the advantaged in the second group but not among the largest in the first, so the consolidating pull helps it in US Fiber Solutions and works against it in Data Center hardware.

  • Toward the largest: Meta and Corning (up to USD 6bn); Molex and Prysmian (up to EUR 5.5bn over ten years); Amphenol buying CommScope CCS (USD 10.5bn); Corning winning 50-60% of optical bids.
  • Toward the advantaged: buyers in subsidised US builds must use domestic product; expert calls credit Hexatronic's lead times, training centres and installation speed; the US recovery was captured disproportionately by local makers with capacity.
  • Implication. Hexatronic's Data Center growth depends on installation and services (labour, certification, relationships), not on owning scarce hardware; its consolidation benefit is regional and cyclical rather than gravitational.
C-28

What is competitors' attitude to competition: do they put margin and return on capital first, or volume first; are they benign and cooperative, or willing to suffer losses; and do industry prices fall when volume falls?

❌ D45

Judgement. Competitors' attitude differs by layer: the upstream glass makers are margin-first and disciplined (a cost to Hexatronic), US duct and cable competitors are currently margin-first because demand exceeds local capacity, and European duct competitors are volume-first and willing to suffer losses; in Europe, prices do fall when volume falls.

  • Upstream: sold out, raising prices, signing long-term hyperscaler contracts, avoiding long fixed-price commitments to telecom operators (Jefferies, October 2026).
  • US ducts and cable: industry-wide price increases in 2026; in 2024-25, lower year-on-year pricing as volumes fell.
  • Europe: inventory dumping at distressed prices, defence of share, structural overcapacity from 2022; prices followed volume down.
  • Data-centre services: customers select on execution, safety, project management and relationships more than on lowest price (Dycom director); large long-haul projects are allocated by route among several contractors.
C-29

How stable is that attitude, and how has it changed over the last eighteen months to three years: do competitors show 'Michael O'Leary' instability (hard to predict, situation-dependent, reactive), and would they launch a 'shark attack' on industry pricing if they sensed weakness?

❌ D38

Judgement. The attitude is unstable and situation-dependent, which is the "Michael O'Leary" pattern: it was hostile in the US during the 2024-25 destock and is benign now; it remains hostile in Europe. A shark attack on pricing is plausible from 2027-28 when the integrated players' new capacity arrives into slower demand growth, and the risk report rates "larger consolidated peers using scale to undercut" as a high-plausibility failure mode.

  • Last 18 months to three years: Europe worsened (overcapacity built in the 2022 boom, still present, low exit); the US improved from destock-era price cuts to 2026 price rises.
  • Triggers for a shark attack: Corning's 50% US fibre expansion; Prysmian's 40-50% US fibre capacity from H2 2028 (underwritten by about EUR 5bn of hyperscaler contracts); 230m fibre-km of Chinese preform capacity by end-2027; data-centre optical demand growth expected to slow from 2028; a pause in hyperscaler capex would bring it forward.
  • Mitigant: in the near term, scarcity and long hyperscaler contracts make price war irrational for the large players.
C-31

Taken together, how supportive is the industry structure, and how fragile is that support?

❌ D40

Judgement. The industry structure is currently supportive for Hexatronic in the US and in data centres, but the support is cyclical and borrowed: it comes from a temporary scarcity of glass and of local compliant capacity, not from Hexatronic's own structural position. Structurally, Hexatronic sits in the fragmented, competitive downstream layer, buys from a concentrated upstream that includes its competitors, and faces a European market with no pricing discipline. The support is fragile and has an approximate expiry around 2028.

  • Supportive now: scarcity, BABA, rising US prices, hyperscalers prioritising supply.
  • Structural negatives: price-taker on inputs (C-25); price-taker in European ducts; no top-two position; low exit barriers in Europe.
  • Pockets of genuine structure: blown-fibre system selling with installer lock-in; defence certifications with 8-10-year lifecycles; subsea framework with NKT to 2032.
  • Implication. Model industry-supported margin upside only through 2027-28; assume the structure reverts toward its long-run competitive state thereafter.
C-84

What do historical precedents, rules of thumb and the empirical evidence show about how companies in this sector and industry have typically generated share price, financial and operational outperformance, and underperformance, and to what extent does this company fit those patterns?

C55

Judgement. Precedents in cable, fibre and small European hardware show that outperformance comes when a volume upswing meets a cut cost base and estimates are rising while the end-market is not yet fully "believed", and that underperformance comes when a demand pulse fades or new capacity lands, with the share price de-rating one to two quarters before earnings fall. Hexatronic fits the outperformance pattern now (rising estimates, a funded end-market half-believed, operating leverage), and its own 2022-25 history is the base rate for the reversal: the same mechanism took its margin from 16.6% to 8.4% and its multiple from about 27x to about 5x.

  • Sector rules of thumb (Hardware Sector Digest, October 2026): a cohort re-rates once its end market is believed; small specialists are priced on last quarter's order lumpiness; in optics the scarcity seller has a short window and the volume seller a longer one, more exposed to the build itself (Hexatronic is the volume seller, judged "half told"); bolt-ons at 5-8x EBITDA create value while growth-led deals produce write-downs; strategic buyers set the benchmark for installer networks.
  • Capacity-cycle precedents (cable): high-voltage cable makers' shares de-rated one to two quarters before earnings fell in 2016-20; the 2001 telecom bust and the 2022-24 FTTH boom-and-destock show fibre volumes overshoot and then destock for 18-24 months.
  • Wisdom Library patterns that apply: demand-pulse reversion and lazy cyclical extrapolation (do not extrapolate the US rebound); use, refill or price (split the recovery); false friends (acquired growth masking decay; weakened by Q3 organic growth); turnarounds take longer than expected; small-cap growth cracks before large caps (relevant in a discount-rate shock).
  • Hexatronic's own history: 2017-22 compounding (about 33% five-year sales CAGR) built on FTTH; 2023-25 bust (diluted EPS SEK 4.17 to -0.07; consensus FY2026 EPS cut from SEK 5.05 to SEK 1.76); 2026 recovery (shares SEK 24.33 in February to SEK 48.9 high, SEK 40.94 on 22 October; +113.5% over twelve months).
  • Where it fits and where it does not. Fits: operating-leverage recovery with rising estimates (a classic early-to-mid outperformance phase). Does not yet fit the compounding precedent: no gravitational advantage, no capital returns, and the mix pivot is half bought. The most likely trigger of underperformance is a quarter in which US Fiber Solutions growth or Data Center normalisation disappoints, with the multiple falling before earnings.
C-32

How is customer demand behaving: to what extent does it support the thesis, and how fragile is that support?

C62

Judgement. Customer demand supports the thesis for 2026-27: US builders, hyperscalers and defence buyers are all spending and prioritising availability, while European operators and oil-and-gas customers are holding back. The support is moderately fragile because the strongest legs are cyclical (US refill), lumpy (data-centre projects, one large Communication Zone project in Q3) or financing-sensitive (AI), and the company discloses neither order intake nor channel inventory.

  • US carriers: PE-backed tier-2/3 builders restarted with lower rates; transport-network demand rising; Hexatronic sells direct, which reduces but does not remove refill risk.
  • Hyperscalers and enterprise: cloud expected to outgrow enterprise; planning horizons up to five years; projects slip up to six months; customers buy glass direct for long-haul routes.
  • Defence: strong order book in Harsh Environment connectivity; government procurement calendars set timing.
  • Europe: operators shifting capital from passing homes to connecting subscribers; high build costs (about EUR 900 per line in the Netherlands against EUR 300 in Spain) and higher rates slow rollouts; alt-nets consolidating.
  • Energy: oil-and-gas project deferrals continue (dynamic cables).
  • Fragility markers to track: order book (about four months), Data Center Q4 against the first-half run-rate, and North American growth after the easy comparisons end.
C-33

To what extent are the route to market and the intermediaries helping or hindering the company, and how fragile is that?

C60

Judgement. The route to market helps Hexatronic and is one of its better structural features: in the US it sells direct to end customers under multi-year frame agreements and trains and certifies installers, which insulated it from the distributor destock that hurt Clearfield; in data centres it sells products plus on-site services. The fragility lies in Europe, where it also sells through distributors and operators who can dual-source, and in data centres, where hyperscalers increasingly bypass intermediaries and allocate work to large contractors.

  • Supportive: direct US model; frame agreements of three to seven years (Chorus seven years, June 2025; Novos Fiber three years, SEK 400m); training centres for inexperienced alt-nets; day-2 on-site Data Center teams; low customer concentration (largest customer below 4% of 2025 sales; top three 9.68%); Data Center top-ten concentration fell from 50% to 40%.
  • Hindering or fragile: hyperscalers procuring glass directly and approving Tier-2 suppliers; long-haul work allocated by route among large contractors; consolidating private-equity installer platforms; in Q3 2026 a material project for one large Communication Zone customer, with concentration undisclosed.
C-34

How are existing competitors behaving, does that help or hurt the company, and how fragile is the company's position against them?

❌ D42

Judgement. Existing competitors are behaving in a way that helps Hexatronic in the short term (industry price rises, supply discipline) and hurts it in the medium term (scale, glass allocation, capacity additions and portfolio breadth). Its position is robust against duct and microduct specialists in its system niche and in the US, and fragile against the integrated majors in data-centre hardware.

  • Corning: strengthening (bid share 50-60%, capacity +50% in US fibre, long hyperscaler contracts with Meta and Verizon); also Hexatronic's supplier.
  • Prysmian: expanding by acquisition (Channell, Xtera, ACSM, Atkore) and capacity (US fibre), repricing legacy fibre contracts and shifting capacity from telecom to hyperscalers (Digital Solutions margin 23.8% in Q2 2026).
  • Amphenol: broader fibre-and-copper offering after buying CommScope CCS.
  • Dura-Line: strong US volume and profit growth (revenue +23%, EBITDA +34% in Q1 2026), tier-1 focus.
  • European duct makers: price-cutting; Nexans in Scandinavia.
  • High-density data-centre specialists: Fujikura, Lightera, Sterlite with IP Hexatronic lacks.
C-35

To what extent are new entrants (from adjacent markets; standard entrants; orthogonal or innovative entrants) entering or threatening this market, and how much of a threat is each type?

❌ D45

Judgement. New-entrant threat is moderate overall and highest from adjacent players: connector majors and large contractors entering data-centre connectivity and installation, and Chinese exporters in European ducts and fibre. Standard and innovative entrants are a limited threat in the US (BABA, tariffs, capital needs) and a niche threat in data-centre customisation.

  • Adjacent: Amphenol (from connectors into broad data-centre connectivity), high threat in Data Center hardware; contractors such as Dycom and MasTec moving "inside the fence" of data centres, medium threat to services; hyperscalers insourcing installation and procurement, a medium-to-long-term threat.
  • Standard entrants: Chinese fibre and duct exporters, high price threat in Europe, low in the US; installation start-ups and PE platforms, medium threat in services (low entry barriers).
  • Orthogonal or innovative: fast customisers (viaPhoton), multicore and hollow-core specialists, co-packaged-optics component makers, low near-term threat, medium after 2028.
C-36

To what extent are substitutes or alternatives (other ways for customers to solve the problems the company's products solve) a threat, and how is that changing?

C65

Judgement. Substitutes are a limited and changing threat: in dense networks and data centres the substitution runs in fibre's favour (optics replacing copper), while in rural subsidised builds fixed wireless and satellite gained ground when BEAD became technology-neutral. Active electrical cables are a modest substitute for some short-reach data-centre links.

  • Rural: fixed wireless and LEO can win up to 26% of BEAD funds; some states lack funds to reach all locations with fibre (Jefferies), which pushes a downshift to cheaper technologies; this dilutes the US subsidy tailwind.
  • Dense and data-centre: fibre's longevity (up to 30 years), symmetry and capacity keep it dominant; 5G and LEO themselves require fibre backhaul.
  • Short reach: active electrical cables are projected to grow about 43% a year to 2029 in data centres, competing with some passive optical interconnect at very short distances.
  • Trend: substitution threat weakening in Hexatronic's core and data-centre markets; rising only at the rural subsidy margin.
C-37

What are regulators and political actors doing, does it help or hurt the company, and how fragile is that position?

C56

Judgement. Regulators and political actors are helping modestly, mostly from 2027 onward, through US localisation rules and subsidies, defence budgets and European digital and security policy; the support is politically fragile (BEAD was redesigned once already, tariffs can be reversed, European subsidies have not transmitted), and two regulatory items can delay Hexatronic's own plans (BABA compliance for cable waiting on the 2027 South Carolina line; the Hudiksvall permit).

  • Helping: BABA and tariffs protect US duct demand for local makers; defence spending; submarine-cable security; EU sovereignty and procurement localisation potential; UK and German gigabit programmes.
  • Hurting or delaying: BEAD technology neutrality and slow disbursement; permitting bottlenecks (86,402 potential events); FDI screening for defence-adjacent deals (JOWO); Corporate Sustainability Reporting Directive and cybersecurity (NIS2-style) compliance costs, unquantified.
  • Fragility: medium; the most valuable support (BABA and tariffs) depends on US trade policy staying protective.
C-38

On whom in the value chain are disruption forces acting (demand, competition, regulators and political actors, suppliers, any other actor), how is each acting, and how fragile do they leave the company's position?

❌ D45

Judgement. Disruption forces act most strongly on Hexatronic's suppliers and customers, and both effects concentrate power away from it: the preform shortage empowers concentrated glass makers, and the AI build empowers hyperscalers who buy direct and lock up supply. Hexatronic is in the squeezed middle, protected only by its niche positions (installation systems, local compliant capacity, certifications, installation labour), so its position is moderately fragile even though demand is strong.

  • Demand (hyperscalers): AI raises fibre intensity (positive volume) but concentrates buying power, encourages direct glass procurement and dark-fibre deals, and depends on financing that showed a first crack (CoreWeave).
  • Competition: integrated players gain from scarcity and from standard-setting in multicore and hollow-core; consolidation (Amphenol-CommScope) raises scale thresholds.
  • Regulators and politicians: BEAD technology neutrality reduces the fibre share of rural subsidy; localisation rules favour local makers (positive for Hexatronic in the US).
  • Suppliers: preform shortage, multiple price rises, allocation to hyperscalers; non-integrated cable makers "have already failed on guaranteed deliveries" in places; Hexatronic reports glass and connectors restricting growth.
  • Other actors (labour): installer shortage favours labour-saving systems but constrains Hexatronic's own service delivery; automation could deflate installation value later.
C-39

Where relevant or material, how are suppliers behaving: a support to the company, or a source of risk?

❌ D30

Judgement. Suppliers are currently a source of risk, not support: glass fibre and selected connectors are constraining Hexatronic's growth, the largest bare-fibre supplier is also a competitor with long hyperscaler contracts, prices have risen several-fold, and Hexatronic's mitigation (dual sourcing, validating new suppliers, index clauses, price increases) is reactive. Resin, the main duct input, has stabilised at lower levels, which is the one supportive input.

  • Exposure: 72,564 tonnes of plastic resin consumed in 2025; bare fibre sourced from a concentrated pool including Corning; raw-material expenses about 60% of sales in Q2 2026.
  • Mitigation: dual sourcing with category managers, long-term supply contracts, suppliers near production sites, validation of new suppliers (Q3 2026), price increases; no financial hedging of transaction exposure.
  • Supportive suppliers: NKT is a customer-partner rather than a supplier; long-term relationships with alternative suppliers exist on paper, but the Q3 disclosure shows redundancy has not translated into immediate substitutability.
