Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 2

Research requests for the next set

Company: Hexatronic Group AB (HTRO-SE; Nasdaq Stockholm), a Swedish maker of passive fibre-optic infrastructure reporting three segments: Fiber Solutions (microducts, ducts, air-blown fibre cables, connectivity and submarine fibre cable for FTTH, transport and middle-mile networks in Europe, North America and Asia-Pacific), Data Center (high-density cabling and connectivity plus design, installation and integration services for hyperscale, colocation and enterprise data centres, mainly in the United States) and Harsh Environment (dynamic cables, specialist connectors and fibre sensing for defence, offshore energy and marine).

Request: T-P1

Peers: Dura-Line (Orbia; the closest US duct and conduit competitor); Emtelle (private, UK; the closest European blown-fibre microduct competitor); Clearfield (US fibre management, a distributor-led model that went through the same 2023-25 destock); CommScope connectivity business now part of Amphenol (fibre connectivity, with a record of restructuring programmes); Prysmian (Digital Solutions segment; integrated fibre and cable, multi-plant footprint); Corning (Optical Communications; integrated glass-to-connectivity, capacity expansions); Nexans (Scandinavian telecom cable competitor); Belden (connectivity solutions mix); Dycom Industries (US fibre and data-centre installation contractor, a labour-cost comparison for Data Center services). Inputs: What is the split of fixed and variable costs in a polymer duct and microduct extrusion plant, in a fibre-cable assembly plant, and in a data-centre installation services business, as far as it can be established for Hexatronic and the named peers? How did Hexatronic's Fiber Solutions and group margins move with volume in the 2021-22 FTTH boom, the 2023-25 destock and the 2026 US recovery, by quarter and by region, with figures and dates? What did Hexatronic's performance improvement programme (announced 11 September 2025, targeting SEK 110m and reported as delivering SEK 120m of annual savings by Q1 2026) actually remove (plants, roles, overheads), how much of it was reversed when the Clinton, South Carolina downsizing was reversed in October 2026, and what costs re-enter? How much under-used European duct capacity remains after the Weterings closure, and what would a further European consolidation save and cost? How variable are labour costs in Data Center installation services (Communication Zone, Superior Fiber & Data Services), and how do acquired service businesses change the group's operating leverage? How did Dura-Line, Clearfield, CommScope and Prysmian's fibre businesses' margins move with volume in 2023-26, and which of their cost programmes delivered against promises? How much margin did peers lose when new duct or cable capacity was commissioned ahead of demand (Ogden, Utah; Clinton, South Carolina; peer US plants)? How does working capital behave per unit of growth in this business model? Focus: How much of Hexatronic's 2026 operating-profit recovery is structural absorption on a lower fixed-cost base and how much is cyclical volume, and what decremental margin would apply if US FTTH and transport volumes fell back after the 2026 surge. Secondly, whether any material cost lever remains in the European duct footprint.

Request: T-P2

Peers: Corning (glass and preform owner, Hexatronic's supplier and competitor, with long hyperscaler supply agreements); Prysmian (integrated, repricing legacy fibre contracts and shifting capacity to hyperscalers); Dura-Line (US duct peer, tier-1 focus); Emtelle (private, European blown-fibre systems); Clearfield (US fibre products through distributors); Fujikura and its subsidiary AFL (high-density data-centre cabling); Sterlite Technologies (integrated fibre and cable, non-European price setter); Amphenol (connectors, now owning CommScope's connectivity business); Radiall and TE Connectivity (defence and harsh-environment connectors, for Harsh Environment pricing). Operating conditions: the 2021-22 FTTH boom with tight capacity; the 2023-25 post-pandemic destock and European microduct overcapacity; the 2025-26 global glass-fibre and preform shortage with several-fold spot price rises; resin, polymer and freight cost spikes (2021-22 and 2026); the 2026 US fibre build recovery and subsidised BEAD builds under Build America Buy America rules; hyperscaler data-centre construction where buyers prioritise supply over price; the expected arrival of new preform and fibre capacity in 2027-28; periods of strong Swedish krona translation. Inputs: What evidence exists of price increases achieved and held, and of price given back, by Hexatronic in US ducts, European microducts, air-blown fibre cable, data-centre products and services, and harsh-environment connectors, from 2021 to 2026? How do Hexatronic's contracts index to resin and fibre prices, what lags apply (peers cite about three months), and how did gross margin behave through each named condition (company-reported adjusted gross margin was 40.1% in Q2 2025, 37.5% in Q3 2025 and 39.8% in Q3 2026, per the Q3 2026 interim report of 22 October 2026)? Do frame agreements (for example Chorus in New Zealand, seven years from June 2025; Novos Fiber, three years) carry price re-openers? Do certified domestic makers earn a premium in BABA-compliant subsidised builds, and does Hexatronic? Do installers' training, certification and system warranties create switching costs that allow a price premium for air-blown systems, and what do installers, operators and competitors say about it? How do hyperscalers and defence buyers trade price against availability during the shortage, and what happens to that pricing once supply normalises? How did European microduct prices behave as volumes fell in 2023-26? What do the sell-side and customers say about Hexatronic's pricing now? Focus: Whether Hexatronic has any pricing power beyond lagged cost pass-through, and in which segments (data centre, subsea, defence, BABA-compliant US ducts) real price increases are achievable and durable. Secondly, how pricing in European ducts and in data-centre products is likely to behave when new glass and fibre capacity arrives.

Request: T-P3

Why requested: Hexatronic's results are driven by several self-reinforcing and self-limiting dynamics (factory absorption, glass allocation under scarcity, acquisition funding through the share price, and the fibre capacity cycle) that could amplify or reverse the 2026 recovery faster than a linear forecast assumes. Peers: Corning (capacity and allocation decisions under scarcity); Prysmian (capacity reallocation and repricing loop in Digital Solutions); Dura-Line (US duct absorption cycle); Clearfield (destock and restock loop through distributors); Nexans and NKT (capacity-cycle precedents in cable, including the high-voltage overcapacity of 2016-20); HMS Networks (a serial bolt-on acquirer whose share price funded acquisitions); Wize Solutions (private-equity installer roll-up competing for targets). Inputs: The absorption loop: does rising US volume lift Fiber Solutions margins, which funds price competitiveness and capacity retention (the Clinton reversal), which wins more volume, and what reverses it? The scarcity loop: does the glass-fibre shortage lead suppliers to allocate to the largest buyers, constraining non-integrated assemblers and shifting share to integrated makers, or does it let Hexatronic prioritise higher-margin data-centre, subsea and defence orders? The installed-base loop: do networks built with air-blown microduct systems pull through compatible connection and drop products as European operators move from passing homes to connecting them? The data-centre services loop: does installation work lead to on-site day-2 services and re-cabling every two to three years, which pulls through products? The acquisition-funding loop: does a higher share price enable equity-funded acquisitions at 5-7x EBITDA (the SEK 600m directed issue at SEK 38 in May 2026), which add earnings and support the price, and how did this loop reverse at other serial acquirers? The European overcapacity loop: do low exit barriers keep prices falling with volume? The capacity-cycle loop: does scarcity pricing attract capacity additions (Corning, Prysmian, Chinese preform makers) that end it, and when? The working-capital loop: does growth absorb cash that forces equity issuance and dilution? Focus: Which of these loops are operating now, with dated evidence, which is likely to dominate Hexatronic's revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years, and what observable signals would show a reinforcing loop turning into a balancing one.

Request: T-P4

Why requested: Hexatronic's earnings per share is unusually noisy between adjusted EBITA and reported EPS (items affecting comparability, acquisition amortisation, contingent-consideration revaluations, tax-asset assessments and share issuance), and broker EPS is reported on several bases, so the EPS bridge needs its inputs evidenced. Peers: Prysmian (tax rate, acquisition amortisation and adjusted-EPS conventions); Nexans (adjusted-EPS conventions and payout policy); HMS Networks and Lifco (Nordic serial acquirers with earn-outs, for contingent-consideration accounting and dilution); NKT (tax-asset utilisation and hybrid treatment as a Nordic comparison); Belden (amortisation of acquired intangibles in a connectivity business). Inputs: The normal group tax rate by country mix (reported tax rate 31.3% in FY2024; 29.6% in Q3 2026 and 26.7% for the first nine months of 2026, the latter helped by deferred-tax-asset assessments, per the Q3 2026 interim report of 22 October 2026). The run-rate amortisation of acquired intangibles and how it grows with each acquisition. Contingent consideration and acquisition options: the terms of the JOWO earn-out (maximum EUR 7.6m on 2026-2030 earnings) and the Superior earn-out (maximum USD 3m on 2026-29 earnings), how revaluations flow through net financial items (SEK 28m in Q3 2026), and how peers treat them in adjusted EPS. Net interest after the May 2026 equity raise and the use of liquidity for acquisitions. The frequency and size of items affecting comparability (SEK 298m of non-recurring costs in 2025; a net positive SEK 71m in Q3 2026 including a SEK 67m reversal of a Clinton impairment). Share count: the 15,789,474 shares issued in May 2026, incentive dilution (LTIP 2026 about 0.41% of shares, about 2.18% including existing rights, per the EGM notice of 22 July 2026), and the likelihood of further directed issues. Currency translation sensitivity of revenue, EBITA and EPS to the Swedish krona, and the net-investment hedge via foreign-currency borrowing. Capitalised development costs and their amortisation. Dividend policy (acquisitions over dividends; no dividend since the payment for 2022). How each broker covering the stock (Redeye and others) defines adjusted EPS. Focus: What a normal conversion from adjusted EBITA to earnings per share looks like for Hexatronic over the next 18 months to four years, which items are most likely to move it, and how far the brokers' adjusted EPS basis differs from reported EPS.

Layer 2

Translating the foundational components into an operating and financial model for the next 18 months, and 18 months to 3 years out

9 questions in 1 group · 11,968 words · written 24 October 2026, 02:32 to 02:53 UK, Fable, ricky login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a well-reasoned, coherent, deeply understood and accurate operating and financial model for the company for the next 18 months to four years, based on the first set's answers, a base of the company's historical financial and operating performance, and how businesses in the sector and industry outperform or underperform, so as to best help the Consumer achieve their objective, which is to produce a trustworthy judgement regarding the predictability of delivery of the financial forecasts over the next 18 months.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each group judgement with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For this set of questions the score says how positive the likely effect on the company's financial forecasts is for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Hexatronic (HTRO-SE): Second set of questions, the operating and financial model for the next 18 months to four years

Prepared 24 October 2026 for the Investment Analyst who assesses the predictability of the forecasts (third set) and their reflection in guidance, consensus and narrative (fourth set).

Reading notes for the Consumer (read before the answers)

  • Bases used throughout. Revenue and segment figures are the company's own (FY2025 SEK 7,519m; not FactSet's 7,245m or the brokers' 7,543m). The operating line is adjusted EBITA (before items affecting comparability and before amortisation of acquired intangibles). "EBITDA" in my inputs is adjusted EBITA plus depreciation (including depreciation of IFRS 16 right-of-use assets), which is the basis the covering broker uses (Redeye FY2027 EBITDA SEK 1,280m against EBITA about SEK 1,024m). "Reported EPS" is diluted EPS after amortisation of acquired intangibles and after items affecting comparability; "clean EPS" strips the items affecting comparability only.
  • A correction to the first set's bridge. The first set treated the FactSet "EPS (adjusted, brokers' basis)" line as an adjusted figure. The evidence in the EPS-bridge report shows Redeye raised its FY2026 EPS to SEK 2.39 after Q3, a figure that includes the SEK 67m Clinton impairment reversal (about SEK 0.23 after tax); FactSet's FY2025 brokers'-basis EPS of SEK -0.33 is also close to the reported SEK -0.07, not to the FactSet-adjusted SEK 0.80. The consensus EPS basis is therefore, in substance, reported diluted EPS after amortisation of acquired intangibles. My cross-check EPS is on that basis so that the model's flag works.

Financial forecasts

C609 questions
P-0162P-0265P-0348P-0450P-0555P-0655P-0856P-0960P-1060

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The forecasts for the next 18 months rest on one mechanism that is working (fixed-cost absorption on North American volume, on a cost base cut by SEK 120m) and one that is diluting it (a growing share of services and acquired revenue at 12-16% margins with variable costs), and the balance between them sets the shape of the whole model: a strong first half of 2027 (revenue growth of about 20% against easy comparisons, margin 10-11%), a sharp fall in the rate of change from Q2 2027 as the US comparisons harden and Data Center's Q3 2026 timing reverses, and a plateau in the adjusted EBITA margin at about 11% from 2027 that neither further self-help (none left), nor price (pass-through only), nor the acquisition loop (margin-dilutive) can lift to the company's 12% target. Below the operating line the picture is cleaner than 2023-25 but structurally heavier than 2020-22: interest halves again and tax normalises at 25-26%, but a share count 8-10% higher and amortisation that grows with each deal hold reported EPS growth two to three points below EBITA growth, so the 2023 EPS peak of SEK 4.17 is not regained until about 2030 on my base case (SEK 2.38, 2.78, 3.29, 3.58, 3.98 for 2026-30). The feedback loops run reinforcing through mid-2027 and turn balancing in 2028-30 as competitive fibre capacity arrives, which is why my 2029 EPS (about SEK 3.6) sits well below the single-broker consensus (SEK 4.18) while my 2026-28 figures sit on or slightly above it. The record argues for leaning the next two to three quarters up against pre-Q3 consensus and leaning 2028-30 down. Free cash flow after leases covers the SEK 400m a year of acquisitions with little left over until 2028, so the balance sheet de-levers slowly and a capital-return decision is a 2029-30 event. The forecasts are more predictable than in the last cycle because the leverage is lower and the drivers are more diversified, but they remain concentrated in two correlated external channels (US carrier spending and hyperscaler spending) and capped by a supply constraint Hexatronic does not control; the cone around 2027 EPS is roughly SEK 2.1 to 3.3 around a base of 2.78, with the skew modestly negative. For the next Analyst: the two observable tests are Q4 2026 Data Center sales against about SEK 500-550m and North American Fiber Solutions growth in Q2-Q3 2027; the two accounting cautions are that consensus EPS is reported, post-amortisation EPS (so compare like with like) and that reported EPS carries SEK 0.10-0.20 a year of one-off and revaluation noise with a negative skew.

P-01

Fixed-cost operating leverage and the main line items

C62

Question asked After assessing the fixed-cost operating leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, against the last four to six years?

Judgement. Fixed-cost operating leverage is the largest single mechanism in the next 18 months' numbers and it is working in Hexatronic's favour, but three features of this cycle make it a smaller and shorter-lived margin engine than in 2021-22 and a smaller destroyer than in 2023-25. First, the leverage is concentrated in Fiber Solutions (58% of Q3 2026 sales), where incremental and decremental margins on organic volume are 30-40%; Data Center (28%) is about half installation services with variable labour, so its leverage is only 15-20%, and the group's blended incremental margin on organic volume is about 25-28%, not the 30% the first set used. Second, a meaningful part of the margin recovery is depreciation dilution rather than cost efficiency: capex has run at 1.5-3% of sales against depreciation of about 3.9% of sales, so depreciation falls as a share of revenue as sales grow, and this adds roughly 60-80 basis points to the adjusted EBITA margin between 2025 and 2027 without any operating improvement; the subsea and South Carolina investments reverse part of this from 2028. Third, the plant base that carried the 2021-22 peak margins (European ducts) is not coming back into absorption, so the margin ceiling in this cycle is about 11-12%, not 16-17%. For the model: adjusted EBITA margin about 10.1% in 2026, about 11.0% in 2027, a plateau of about 11.3% in 2028 and a drift to about 11.0% in 2029-30 as competitive capacity lands and the service mix rises; and a cone around 2027 EBITA of roughly plus or minus SEK 200m (plus or minus 19%) from a plus or minus 10% swing in Fiber Solutions organic volume alone.

How the leverage decomposes, segment by segment.

  • Fiber Solutions (manufacturing, high leverage). The cost structure of a duct or microduct plant is about 60-65% of sales in raw material (variable), 5% direct labour, 4% energy, and the rest plant fixed costs, overhead and depreciation (Jefferies, September 2020, confirmed by the Hexatronic Q2 2026 raw-material ratio of 61% of group sales). Incremental margins on organic volume are therefore about 35%: the Q3 2026 year-on-year evidence is sales up about SEK 174m organically and adjusted EBITA up SEK 66m (38%), with gross margin up 230 basis points and the operating-cost ratio down 120 basis points at group level. The decremental side was shown in 2023-25, when Fiber Solutions sales fell from about SEK 6.4bn (2022) to SEK 4.87bn (2025) and the segment margin fell from the high teens to 6.4% (5.2% in Q4 2025), with price declines compounding volume under-absorption. The segment's margin went 5.4% (Q3 2025), 5.2% (Q4), 6.2% (Q1 2026), 9.1% (Q2), 9.4% (Q3): the first two steps were the SEK 120m programme landing, the last two were North American volume (plus 44% in Q2, plus 51% in Q3) absorbing retained capacity (the Clinton reversal). The European plants remain under-absorbed (Fiber Solutions Europe -31%, -10%, -9% in Q1-Q3 2026); their recovery is not in my base case, which is the single largest latent upside to leverage and also the reason the cycle peak is lower.
  • Data Center (services-heavy, low leverage). Services were about 40-50% of segment sales (management, October 2025; Redeye, September 2025) before the Communication Zone and Superior acquisitions, which are almost wholly services; the share is now probably above half. Installation labour and subcontractors are variable (Dycom's cost of earned revenue is 79% of revenue, scaled through subcontractors), so the segment's incremental margin on volume is low (15-20%) but so is its decremental margin. The evidence fits: the margin held in a 15.3-16.9% band through five quarters in which quarterly sales doubled (SEK 334m to SEK 692m), and Superior's 12.5% standalone margin did not dent the Q3 margin because product sales (higher margin) rose with it. The consequence for the group is that the fastest-growing segment adds revenue with little operating leverage, so group margin expansion from here depends mainly on Fiber Solutions absorption and Harsh Environment mix, not on Data Center growth.
  • Harsh Environment (plant-based, medium leverage, lumpy). Incremental margins about 25-30%; the segment margin is held at 10-11% by the oil-and-gas deferrals at Rochester and Fibron (dynamic cables) while defence connectivity runs at record levels. A dynamic-cable recovery would show high incremental margins on an under-absorbed plant base; the 2028 Hudiksvall subsea line adds about SEK 300m of fixed assets and, on Redeye's estimates, about SEK 325m of sales at a 20% margin from 2028-29.

The depreciation-dilution point (new to this set). FactSet total depreciation and amortisation was SEK 402m in 2025, of which about SEK 108m was amortisation of acquired intangibles, leaving about SEK 290m of depreciation (including right-of-use assets), 3.9% of sales. Capex was SEK 94m in 2025 and is running at about SEK 140m in 2026. Depreciation therefore drifts flat to slightly down while revenue rises 15% in 2026 and about 11% in 2027, so depreciation falls to about 3.3% of sales in 2026 and 3.1% in 2027. Of the roughly 260 basis points of adjusted EBITA margin recovery from 2025 (8.4%) to 2027 (about 11.0%), about 70 basis points is this arithmetic. It is real profit, but it is not evidence of pricing or efficiency, and it reverses by about 20-30 basis points in 2028-29 when the subsea line and the South Carolina cable line are commissioned and depreciation rises to about SEK 335-360m.

Against the last four to six years. 2021-22: group revenue rose 68% and 88%, the adjusted EBITA margin reached 16.6% (2022) and a quarterly peak of 17.2% (Q1 2023), on tight European and US capacity, rising duct prices and full plants. 2023-25: revenue fell 8% from the 2023 peak while adjusted EBITA fell 49% (SEK 1,234m to SEK 632m), a decremental margin near 100% because price fell with volume and the 2021-23 capacity build (SEK 462-510m of capex a year) had raised the fixed base. This cycle: revenue growth of about 15% in 2026 is half acquired (acquired sales carry 12-15% margins and little leverage) and the organic half is split between Fiber Solutions (high leverage) and Data Center (low leverage), so group adjusted EBITA rises about 39% in 2026 on 15% revenue growth, an incremental margin of about 20% on total revenue growth and about 28% on organic growth. That is why the margin lands at 10% in 2026 rather than snapping back to the mid-teens.

What it does to each line item, 2026-30 against 2020-25.

  • Revenue: volume-led, 15% (2026), about 11-12% (2027, including a modest allowance for unannounced acquisitions), 10-12% (2028, with the acquisition block and the subsea line), 7-10% (2029-30).
  • Gross margin (adjusted): about 39-40% in 2026-27 (absorption plus pass-through), easing to 38-39% in 2029-30 as the service mix rises and capacity arrives; the 41-42% of 2022-24 is not reached because the mix has moved to services.
  • Operating costs: 25.0% of sales (Q3 2026) falling to about 24% by 2028 through scale, not cuts.
  • Depreciation: SEK 290m (2026), 305m (2027), 335m (2028), 360m (2029), 375m (2030).
  • Adjusted EBITA margin: 10.1%, 11.0%, 11.3%, 10.9%, 11.0%.
  • EBITDA margin (EBITA plus depreciation): 13.4%, 14.1%, 14.4%, 14.0%, 13.9%.

Sensitivities the Consumer should carry. A plus or minus 10% swing in Fiber Solutions organic volume in 2027 (about SEK 520m of sales) moves group adjusted EBITA by about SEK 160-200m (15-19% of my 2027 EBITA of SEK 1,057m) and EPS by about SEK 0.55-0.65. The same swing in Data Center (about SEK 265m) moves EBITA by about SEK 45-55m. A 5% volume loss in Fiber Solutions Europe alone (about SEK 100m) costs about SEK 35-40m. In a downside in which North American Fiber Solutions volume falls 20% in 2027 (the refill ending) and Data Center grows only 5%, group adjusted EBITA would be about SEK 850-900m (9.5-10%) on revenue of about SEK 8.9-9.0bn, and reported EPS about SEK 2.1; this is the scenario the third set should test against the base case.

Peers for calibration. Dura-Line (Orbia Connectivity Solutions) runs a 15.7% EBITDA margin (2025) and 16.9% (Q2 2026) at about 65% US conduit share, against Hexatronic's Fiber Solutions EBITDA margin of about 8.9% (2025); management itself attributes the gap to utilisation and key-account pricing, so Hexatronic's US leverage has room to run if volume holds. Prysmian Digital Solutions went from 16.8% to 23.8% EBITDA margin in four quarters on hyperscaler mix and repricing; Clearfield's gross margin went from 35.7% to 13.7% and back to 31.8% through the destock, the clearest decremental-margin precedent in the cohort. Hexatronic sits between these: more leverage than a service business, less upside than an integrated maker.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set's 30% group incremental margin should be read as 25-28% once the services mix is weighted, and the first set did not separate the depreciation-dilution effect; both differences lower the margin that pure volume growth can deliver by 2028 and are already in my inputs.

P-02

Financial leverage, earnings per share and the balance sheet

C65

Question asked After assessing the financial leverage in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share and the balance sheet over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Financial leverage is low, falling and, for the next 18 months, a modest tailwind to EPS rather than a risk: net debt excluding leases was SEK 1,305m at Q3 2026 (adjusted leverage 1.3x) and should be about SEK 1.1bn at end-2026 and end-2027 even after about SEK 400m a year of acquisitions, because free cash flow after leases of about SEK 400-600m a year covers the acquisition cadence. Net interest falls from SEK 200m (2024) and SEK 151m (2025, FactSet) to about SEK 105m (2026) and SEK 80-95m (2027-28), which is worth about SEK 0.35 of EPS cumulatively from 2024 to 2028, roughly 10% of 2028 EPS; the years-on-year EPS contribution is 2-3% in 2027 and 2028. The balance sheet becomes under-levered by 2029 (net debt to EBITDA about 0.3x on my base case) unless acquisitions step up or a dividend begins, which is why I pencil a first dividend into 2030 and why the realistic risk is a larger, equity-funded acquisition rather than distress. Over four years the balance sheet therefore amplifies neither upside nor downside much; its contribution to EPS is a slow positive from falling interest, partly offset by rising amortisation of acquired intangibles and by earn-out revaluations that add plus or minus SEK 30m a quarter of noise to financial items.

The numbers.

  • Debt and liquidity. Net debt excluding leases: SEK 3,094m (September 2023), 1,582m (end-2025), 1,672m (Q1 2026), 1,470m (Q2), 1,305m (Q3). Lease liabilities about SEK 475m (FactSet lease-inclusive net debt SEK 1,946m at Q2 against the company's SEK 1,470m). Available liquidity SEK 2,090m. Covenants exclude IFRS 16 and have ample headroom. Borrowing is held in USD 88m, GBP 26m and EUR 8m as a net-investment hedge, so a stronger krona reduces reported debt as well as reported earnings.
  • Cost. Net interest SEK 81m for nine months 2026 (SEK 26m in Q3, from 31m a year earlier). Net financial items are noisier: SEK -102m for nine months, including SEK -10m of revaluations of contingent consideration and acquisition options (SEK -28m in Q3 alone, against SEK +35m for nine months 2025). My inputs: net financial items SEK -132m (2026), -105m (2027), -95m (2028), -80m (2029), -65m (2030), each including about SEK 20m of lease interest and a SEK 10m allowance for revaluation charges, because acquisitions that outperform generate revaluation losses.
  • Equity. SEK 4,896m at Q3 2026 after the SEK 600m directed issue (15,789,474 shares at SEK 38, May 2026); goodwill and customer relationships dominate assets. Return on equity rises from about 11% (2026) toward 14% (2028) on my base case, still below the level that justifies a premium multiple.
  • Path. End-2026 net debt excluding leases about SEK 1.1bn (0.9x my EBITDA); end-2027 about SEK 1.1bn (0.8x); end-2028 about SEK 0.9bn (0.55x); end-2029 about SEK 0.5bn (0.3x); end-2030 about SEK 0.2bn after a first dividend. The model will compute its own path from my inputs; these are my cross-check figures.

How it shapes EPS. The EBITA-to-EPS conversion improves mainly because interest falls while EBITA rises: net interest was 24% of adjusted EBITA in 2025 (SEK 151m on 632m), about 12% in 2026 and about 9% in 2027-28. The conversion of adjusted EBITA to reported net income is about 58% in 2026, 59% in 2027, 61% in 2028 and 62% in 2029, against 70% in 2023 (SEK 848m on 1,234m) when amortisation was lower and interest had not yet risen; the gap to 2023 is amortisation of acquired intangibles (SEK 100-135m a year, growing with each deal) and a higher tax rate.

Policy and risk. Leverage is a lever for acquisitions, not for shareholder returns: three to six deals a year under EUR 50m at 5-7x EBITDA, financed from cash flow and facilities, with directed equity for larger steps. At 2.0x leverage the company could borrow roughly SEK 1.5-2.0bn more by 2028 on my EBITDA path, enough for a transformational deal; a repeat of the May 2026 pattern (a directed issue at a discount) is the main dilution risk, which I put at about 30% over the four years. The second risk is accounting rather than financial: the CFO transition coincides with earn-outs (JOWO up to EUR 7.6m to 2030; Superior up to USD 3m to 2029) whose revaluations flow through financial items and whose cash settlement (SEK 527m of business-combination outflows in nine months 2026, including earn-outs on KNET, DCS and Qubix) falls in 2029-31.

Against peers. HMS Networks de-levered from 2.8x to 1.6x in a year on cash conversion above 100% while continuing bolt-ons; Lifco keeps option liabilities in equity to shield EPS. Hexatronic's cash conversion (72-84% in 2026 quarters) is weaker, so its de-leveraging relies more on EBITDA growth and equity, but the direction is the same.

Where I differ from the first set. None of substance; I add that the balance sheet is on course to be under-levered by 2029, which makes a capital-return decision or a larger acquisition a 2029-30 event the fourth set should watch for.

P-03

Cost elimination and efficiency: how much reaches the line items, and when

❌ D48

Question asked After assessing the scope for cost elimination and efficiency in greater detail, how much is likely to reach the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, and when?

Judgement. The self-help is finished and almost entirely in the numbers already: the SEK 120m programme (SEK 110m targeted, delivered ahead of schedule by Q1 2026) is in the 2026 run-rate, so the year-on-year benefit to 2026 against 2025 is about SEK 90-100m (part landed in H2 2025) and the incremental benefit to 2027 against 2026 is only SEK 10-20m. What remains is scale efficiency, not elimination: operating costs fall from 25.0% of sales (Q3 2026) toward about 24% by 2028 because revenue grows faster than central and selling costs, worth about 100 basis points of margin over three years, offset by about 30-40 basis points of cost re-entry (Clinton fixed costs and depreciation reinstated, South Carolina cable-line start-up in 2027, technician wage inflation of 4%-plus in services, and public-company and compliance costs that grow with acquisitions). A further European duct consolidation (SEK 50-100m, an inference) is the one material lever left and I do not include it, because management has moved Fiber Solutions "from restructuring to growth" and because Europe's shift to connection products needs the plants. Net: cost efficiency contributes about 50-70 basis points to the 2027-28 margin, all of it from scale; there is no second programme in the base case, and the probability of one within 18 months is low (about 15%) unless US volume reverses, in which case it would be a defensive cut with a 12-18-month lag to the accounts.

What was cut and what came back.

  • Programme: Weterings (Netherlands) closed with 120 roles removed; European microduct production consolidated to Sweden and Austria; Fiber Solutions simplified to four regions plus global product and sourcing; executive team cut from eight to seven; Clinton, South Carolina downsized (SEK 10m of the SEK 120m) with SEK 67m of restructuring cost, of which SEK 58m was a non-cash impairment. Cash restructuring costs were about SEK 298m in 2025 (all non-recurring items), so the payback was under three years.
  • Reversal: in Q3 2026 the Clinton downsizing was reversed and the SEK 67m impairment written back; depreciation on those assets (roughly SEK 8-10m a year) and the related fixed costs return to the base. Redeye calls the reversal "rare and highly positive"; it is positive for revenue capacity and negative for cost flexibility, and it is the clearest evidence that capacity decisions at this company swing with the cycle.

What is cuttable and what is not.

  • Raw materials (about 60% of sales) are pass-through, not cuttable; new-supplier validation (glass and connectors) is about availability and may raise unit cost in the short run.
  • European duct footprint: Fiber Solutions Europe is still falling 9% a year; a second consolidation would save perhaps SEK 50-100m but would cost SEK 100-150m in cash and would remove capacity that the connection-product shift and any German or UK recovery would need. It is the lever a new CEO uses when the US turns down, not one he uses while North America grows 51%.
  • Services labour is variable and scarce; cutting it is not efficiency, it is lost revenue.
  • Overhead in acquired entities: the decentralised model ("freedom with responsibility", founders retained) rules out central integration savings by design; CommScope NEXT and Orbia's USD 250m programme show that centralised cuts in this sector take two to four years and USD 200m of charges to deliver, a path Hexatronic has not chosen.

Timing to the line items.

  • 2026: full-year effect of the programme (about SEK 90-100m year on year), visible as the fall in Fiber Solutions' operating-cost ratio and the segment margin from 5.4% to 9.4%; it is complete.
  • 2027: SEK 10-20m of residual programme benefit; scale efficiency of about SEK 40-60m as revenue grows 11%; cost re-entry of about SEK 30-40m (Clinton, South Carolina start-up, wage inflation). Net about SEK 20-40m, or 20-40 basis points.
  • 2028-30: scale only, about 30 basis points a year gross, half consumed by inflation and compliance.

Peer precedents. Prysmian closed Calais, Washington (UK) and disengaged from Battipaglia in 2023-24 and its Digital Solutions margin recovered only when hyperscaler volume arrived; Orbia's programme reached USD 200m of USD 250m by end-2025 while Dura-Line's margin still depended on volume; Belden's re-organisation into a functional model (January 2026) protected a 39.6% gross margin by mix rather than by cuts. The sector's lesson is that cuts stabilise the trough and volume sets the peak; Hexatronic is now in the volume phase.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set (C-77) listed SEK 50-100m of further European consolidation as cuttable; I agree it exists but I exclude it from the base case and classify it as a downside-scenario response, because its timing is contingent on the US cycle turning, not on management choice today.

P-04

Pricing power and the contribution of price to revenue and margin

C50

Question asked After assessing the pricing power in greater detail, how is price likely to contribute to revenue and margin over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Price is a pass-through variable at Hexatronic, not a profit driver, and the model should treat it as such: it contributes about plus 1-2 points to 2026 and 2027 revenue growth (US duct and cable increases "with the industry", fibre and transport surcharges), roughly nothing in 2028, and minus 1-2 points in 2029-30 when new preform and fibre capacity (China's 230m fibre-km by end-2027, Corning's plus 50%, Prysmian's doubling of US fibre from H2 2028) removes scarcity pricing in data-centre products and lets European duct prices resume their decline. Its effect on margin is close to neutral through 2027, because every increase is cost-matched with a one-quarter lag (index-linked duct contracts; glass bought on longer agreements), and the lag runs both ways: when input costs fall in 2028-29 the same indexation returns the gain to customers after a quarter, so the gross margin receives a brief tailwind and then settles. Real pricing above cost exists only in defence connectors, subsea cable and, for as long as the shortage lasts, in data-centre hardware where hyperscalers prioritise availability; these are about 15-20% of sales, so even a 3-5% real price premium there adds only 50-100 basis points to the group gross margin, which is the most I would credit, and only for 2026-27. The company does not disclose price magnitudes, so none of this is observable directly; the gross margin (39.8% in Q3 2026) is the only instrument, and I hold it at 39-40% through 2027 and let it drift to 38-39% by 2030.

The evidence by segment and condition.

  • US ducts and cable (about 30% of sales). 2024-25: duct prices lower year on year, offset by volume; 2026: industry-wide increases, passed through "backed by industry-wide pricing hikes" (CEO), with resin indexation lags of three to four months (the Dura-Line precedent from 2021). Dura-Line sets price at 65% share; Hexatronic (about 10% US duct share, Redeye 2024) follows. BABA-compliant domestic makers are said to earn a 10-20% premium in subsidised builds (Calix, July 2025), but the AlphaSense pricing report finds the premium "completely unquantified" and Redeye says localisation "does not guarantee a direct price premium"; Hexatronic's cable is not BABA-compliant until the South Carolina line runs in 2027. I credit a small, unquantified premium on ducts in BEAD builds from 2027, worth perhaps SEK 20-40m of margin a year by 2028, and nothing on cable before 2028.
  • European microducts (about 20% of sales). Prices fell with volume through 2023-25 and Q4 2025 (volume up, price down, margin 5.2%); overcapacity from the 2022 boom persists, exit barriers are low in theory but extruders stay because equipment has no liquidation value; Chinese fibre exports depressed pricing. I model European price flat to down 1-2% a year throughout, with volume flat to down.
  • Data Center (28%). Hyperscalers and colocation builders prioritise speed to power and availability over price (Jefferies, AWS procurement, September-October 2026); Fujikura has taken about 25% like-for-like increases on uncommitted capacity (Morgan Stanley, August 2026); Corning's capacity is committed to Meta, NVIDIA and AT&T, so it has little spot upside and Hexatronic, a volume seller, has some. Against this, Google keeps at least three active suppliers for critical optical components and Tier-1 carriers are moving 15-20% of fibre spend to lower-cost suppliers (Morgan Stanley, October 2026). Services are priced by project on execution, not on scarcity. I model data-centre product price plus 3-5% in 2026-27, flat in 2028, minus 3-5% in 2029-30; services price with wage inflation.
  • Harsh Environment (16%). Defence buyers accept premiums for traceability and certified origin (Rheinmetall programme manager, November 2025); connector lead times are up about 20% (RTX, October 2026); the counter is a shift to commercial-off-the-shelf parts (Radiall, October 2026). Amphenol's Harsh Environment margin is 30.1%; Hexatronic's 11% says its position is in lower-value niches and in dynamic cables where oil-and-gas buyers hold the power. Real price plus 2-3% a year in defence connectors is plausible and I include it in the Harsh Environment margin path (11% to 14-14.5% by 2029-30, with mix and the subsea line).
  • Subsea. The NKT framework to 2032 fixes volume for a significant portion of the doubled Hudiksvall capacity; the margin (Redeye about 20%) reflects a sole-source line, not spot pricing.

How pass-through shaped the gross margin, and what it implies. Adjusted gross margin: 41.4% (Q4 2024), 40.1% (Q2 2025), 37.5% (Q3 and Q4 2025), 38.7% (Q2 2026), 39.8% (Q3 2026). The 2025 fall was European volume and price, not input costs; the 2026 recovery is absorption plus mix plus pass-through, and the CFO's July warning of Q3 margin pressure from raw materials did not materialise. The reading is that the pass-through machine works at a one-quarter lag in both directions, so the gross margin is a function of volume and mix, and price is noise around it. The one scenario in which price becomes a margin driver is a second step up in glass prices (spot rose five-fold in China and 3.6x in Europe since July 2025) that a supplier allocates away from Hexatronic: then price would be given on volume lost rather than margin lost. That is a volume risk dressed as a price risk, and it is in my downside scenario, not the base case.

Against the last four to six years. 2021-22 was the only period of real pricing (tight capacity, duct prices up, gross margin above 40% with full plants); 2023-25 gave it all back in Europe. This cycle gives Hexatronic cost recovery and a brief scarcity window in data-centre hardware, but no structural pricing, because the concentrated profit pool (preform and glass, where Corning, YOFC, ZTT, Furukawa and Prysmian hold about 67% of capacity) is upstream of it and because its own layer is fragmented.

Where I differ from the first set. None of substance; I add the two-way lag point (falling input costs in 2028-29 produce a temporary gross-margin tailwind of perhaps 50-100 basis points for one or two quarters, which the Consumer should not mistake for structural pricing) and I quantify the real-pricing pockets at 15-20% of sales.

P-05

Reinforcing and balancing feedback loops and the main line items

C55

Question asked After assessing in greater detail whether reinforcing or balancing feedback loops are at work in the business model, such that dynamics at play today create additional scope for further positive/negative dynamics in the near/mid-term (reinforcing feedback loops), or the opposite (balancing loops), how are they likely to shape the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. Three reinforcing loops are running now and will dominate the next 18 months' revenue and margin; four balancing loops are already visible and will dominate 2028-30. The reinforcing loops are (1) the US absorption loop (North American volume lifts Fiber Solutions margin toward 10%, which funds capacity retention and competitive pricing, which wins more volume as BEAD orders begin in 2027), (2) the data-centre services loop (installation leads to day-2 services, re-cabling and product pull-through, with services about half of segment sales and hyperscalers now demanding on-site support), and (3) the acquisition-funding loop (a share at about 13.8x forward earnings buys service companies at 5-7x EBITDA, adds EPS and supports the share, as it has at HMS Networks for fifteen years). The balancing loops are (4) the scarcity loop (glass and connectors cap volume growth and tie up working capital; the allocation benefit to Hexatronic's higher-margin orders is unproven), (5) the European overcapacity loop (prices fall with volume, exit is slow), (6) the working-capital loop (growth absorbs about SEK 230-260m per SEK 1bn of revenue, which is why free cash flow lags earnings), and (7) the capacity-cycle loop (scarcity pricing has attracted Corning, Prysmian, Sterlite, HFCL and Chinese preform capacity that lands from late 2027 and ends the scarcity, with data-centre optical demand growth expected to slow from 2028). The net effect is a profile the model should carry explicitly: reinforcing in 2026-27 (margin rising with volume), balanced in 2028 (volume still growing, price support fading), and mildly balancing in 2029-30 (margin drifting down 30-50 basis points as capacity lands while the acquisition loop keeps revenue growing). The signal that turns the reinforcing loops balancing is observable: a fall in G.652.D spot fibre prices and in lead times, about one quarter before it reaches Hexatronic's gross margin.

Each loop, how it works, and the evidence it is running.

  • US absorption loop (reinforcing, strongest now). Volume (North America plus 44% in Q2, plus 51% in Fiber Solutions in Q3) lifts utilisation at Clinton, Ogden and the Blue Diamond plants; the segment margin rose 5.2% to 9.4% in three quarters; management reversed the Clinton downsizing to hold capacity; retained capacity and short lead times win share (Hexatronic grew while larger US peers declined in 2024-25). What reverses it: price erosion overpowering volume (it did in Q4 2025), a refill ending (my estimate 20-25% probability over 18 months), or BEAD's fibre share falling further (30% of BEAD now toward fixed wireless and satellite). The loop strengthens if BEAD orders arrive on schedule in 2027 and the South Carolina cable line makes cable BABA-compliant.
  • Data-centre services loop (reinforcing, medium strength, lower margin). Communication Zone delivered a material project for a large customer in Q3; Superior broadened the customer base into education, healthcare and government; hyperscalers require on-site installation and testing support (former Meta procurement manager, July 2026); Redeye cites re-cabling every two to three years. The counter-evidence is Morgan Stanley's CIO survey (October 2026) showing data-centre switching replacement cycles lengthening to 4.7 years, and the fact that renewal rates for Hexatronic's services are not disclosed. I treat the loop as real for revenue (it underpins my 15% organic Data Center growth in 2027 and 10-12% in 2028) and neutral for margin (services dilute to 15-15.5%).
  • Acquisition-funding loop (reinforcing, conditional on the multiple). SEK 600m raised at SEK 38 funded Superior at 5.8-6.4x; JOWO at 4.7x including earn-outs; Nordic serial acquirers paying 6-8x earn 13-17% steady-state returns (DNB Carnegie). HMS Networks shows the loop running for fifteen years (17 acquisitions, 9.7x paid against 26x own multiple) and de-levering between deals. It reverses when the acquirer's multiple falls toward the target multiples (serial acquirers traded at about 10x EV/EBIT in downturns) or when integration fails (Clarivate). Hexatronic's 13.8x forward P/E keeps the arbitrage open; below about 9x it closes, and private-equity roll-ups (Wize Solutions) are already lifting multiples for larger service targets. I keep SEK 400m a year of acquisitions in the base case for this reason and would remove them in any scenario in which the share de-rates by a third.
  • Scarcity loop (balancing now; two-sided). Glass and connectors "were beginning to restrict growth" (22 October 2026), with backlog and lead-time extensions; non-integrated European fabricators are the most exposed (Jefferies); Corning limits glass sales to other assemblers (October 2025). The possible positive (prioritising data-centre, subsea and defence orders) is unproven. In the model it caps 2027 organic growth below demand (I use 9% against demand indicators above 12%) and adds about one point of working capital to sales.
  • European overcapacity loop (balancing, structural). Fiber Solutions Europe -31%, -10%, -9%; Pantheon Infrastructure withdrew from German FTTH (September 2026); operators move from passing to connecting homes; microduct demand down and price pressure continuing. The installed-base pull-through of connection products cushions but does not grow Europe; the loop keeps European Fiber Solutions margins near break-even and caps the segment at about 10%. Reversal would need capacity exits by competitors or a regulatory lift ("regulation light", Bernstein), neither of which I credit before 2028.
  • Working-capital loop (balancing, cash not profit). Net working capital 26.1% of sales at Q2 2026; nine-month outflow SEK 205m; cash conversion 72% in Q3. Growth of SEK 1bn a year absorbs SEK 230-260m, so free cash flow after leases runs at 60-90% of net income in growth years; Redeye warned (October 2024) that this can force equity raises, and it did in May 2026. The model carries working capital at 24% of year-end sales in 2026-27, easing to 23% by 2029 as service revenue (lower inventory) grows.
  • Capacity-cycle loop (balancing from late 2027). The precedents are exact: Chinese fibre overcapacity after the 2015-17 expansion halved loose-fibre prices in 18 months (2018-21); high-voltage cable overcapacity in 2016-20 cut Nexans and NKT margins and forced impairments; Corning is adding 50%, Prysmian EUR 1.25bn through 2031 for US fibre, Sterlite plus 50%, HFCL doubling by October 2028, and China 230m fibre-km of preform by end-2027. Bernstein (16 October 2026) already reports spot tightness subsiding. For a non-integrated assembler the first effect is positive (input costs fall, availability returns) and the second is negative (scarcity pricing in data-centre hardware ends; European price pressure deepens as Chinese cable seeks export markets). I net these to a 30-50 basis-point margin drift down in 2029-30 and lower price in revenue.

Which loop dominates when. 2026 to mid-2027: the absorption loop (Fiber Solutions margin from 8.7% to about 9.5-10%) and the services loop (Data Center revenue plus 15-20%) dominate revenue and margin; the scarcity and working-capital loops cap volume and cash. Mid-2027 to 2028: the acquisition loop and the subsea step carry revenue; absorption flattens as comparisons harden. 2029-30: the capacity-cycle loop dominates margin; the acquisition loop and defence carry revenue.

Signals that a reinforcing loop has turned. North American Fiber Solutions growth falling into single digits before Q2 2027 (refill over); Data Center quarterly sales below about SEK 520m in Q4 2026 or Q1 2027 (normalisation harder than guided); G.652.D spot fibre prices falling and lead times shortening (scarcity ending, about one quarter ahead of margin); a forward P/E below about 10x (acquisition loop closing); working capital above 26% of sales at a year-end (cash loop binding).

Where I differ from the first set. The first set (C-47) found gravitational forces weak and the two mild mechanical forces to be installed-base pull-through and mix; I agree, and I add that the three reinforcing loops above are all contingent on external conditions (US volume, hyperscaler spending, the share price) rather than self-sustaining, which is why I model them as fading rather than compounding.

P-06

The track record of delivery: how much weight the forecasts carry, and which way to lean

C55

Question asked After assessing the track record of delivery in greater detail, how much weight should the forecasts for the next 18 months to four years carry, and in which direction should they be leaned?

Judgement. The record splits cleanly into a poor mid-term record (2022-25: margins from 16.6% to 8.4%, FY2026 consensus EPS cut from SEK 5.05 to SEK 1.76, four misses and a profit warning, four capacity-commissioning slips) and a strong eighteen-month record under the current chief executive (cost programme over-delivered early, four of five quarters beaten, the Q3 2026 beat of 21% on EBITA and 37% on EPS against the company-commissioned consensus, cautious cues that proved conservative). The weight the forecasts deserve therefore depends on the horizon. For the next two to three quarters (Q4 2026 to Q2 2027) the forecasts deserve moderate-to-high weight and should be leaned up against the pre-Q3 consensus, because management's cues have under-stated outcomes for a year, because Q1 2027 meets an exceptionally easy comparison (SEK 1,698m of sales, 8.6% margin, organic -2% in a cold winter), and because the order book covers about four months. For 2027 as a whole the weight is moderate and the lean is neutral-to-slightly-up on revenue and neutral on margin: the Data Center normalisation and the hardening of US comparisons from Q2 2027 are known, and the supply cap is live. For 2028 the weight is low-to-moderate and the lean is neutral on revenue (the SEK 11bn target is reachable only with acquisitions, which my base case includes) and down on margin (the LTIP range of 9.8-11.1% average margin over 2026-28 is the board's own planning anchor and my path averages 10.8%). For 2029-30 the weight is low and the lean is clearly down against the single-broker consensus (EPS SEK 4.18 in 2029 rests on an EBIT margin step to 12.1% and falling depreciation, neither of which the loops support; my 2029 EPS is about SEK 3.6). The company's own history says the turn, when it comes, is faster than the forecasts: in 2023 the multiple fell before earnings and estimates were cut by two-thirds over two years, so the forecasts should carry an explicit downside path, not just a wider band.

The evidence, dated.

  • Beats and misses against the company-commissioned consensus. Q3 2025 sales +6%; Q4 2025 sales +11%, EBITA beat; Q1 2026 sales -6% (weather) but EBITA SEK 146m against 140m and EPS SEK 0.45 against 0.30; Q2 2026 sales +11%, EBITA +10-12%, EPS SEK 0.61 against 0.49; Q3 2026 sales +11% (SEK 2,498m against 2,256m), EBITA +21% (SEK 281m against 233m), EPS +37% (SEK 0.81 against 0.59, flattered by the one-off). Four profit beats in five quarters, each larger than the last; the pattern is the classic early-to-mid upswing in a company whose cost base was cut before volume returned.
  • Qualitative cues against outcomes. July 2026: "temporary pressure on margins in the third quarter" from raw materials; outcome, margin 11.3% and gross margin up 230 basis points. September 2025: targets rebased down, FTTH outlook softened; outcome, a 2026 recovery above every forecast. October 2026: Q4 Data Center to normalise toward the first-half pattern, Q4 and Q1 seasonally lower, submarine similar to Q3, US growth continuing, Europe subdued, glass and connectors constraining. On the record, these cues are more likely to be under- than over-statements, which is why my Q4 2026 (sales SEK 2,230m, margin 10.1%) sits above a mechanical reading of them.
  • Targets. 2028: SEK 11bn, about 12% EBITA margin, with segment targets (Fiber Solutions SEK 6bn at 10%, Data Center SEK 3bn at 15%, Harsh Environment SEK 2bn at 15%), acquisitions included. Held, not raised, through three beats. My base case reaches about SEK 10.8bn in 2028 at 11.3%, with Data Center at about SEK 3.0bn (target met), Fiber Solutions about SEK 5.4bn plus most of the acquired block (about SEK 0.3-0.4bn short), and Harsh Environment about SEK 1.8bn at 13% (short on both counts). The 12% group margin needs Harsh Environment at 15%, which needs a dynamic-cable recovery that the files do not show.
  • Mid-term failures. Clinton start-up delayed two months (2022); South Carolina full operation deferred to late Q3 2023; Ogden postponed from Q2 to Q3 2024; South Carolina cable lines deferred to 2027; BEAD expectations pushed from H2 2025 to H2 2026 and now to 2027. Capacity and subsidy timing is where this company has consistently been late, which is why I phase the subsea line's contribution from mid-2028 rather than January 2028 and treat BEAD as 2027-28 upside.
  • The sell-side. One visible broker on FactSet (Redeye), four to five contributors to the company's consensus; estimates rose 12-27% over twelve months and then paused (one- and three-month revisions near zero before Q3). Redeye's post-Q3 EPS: SEK 2.39 (2026), about 3.36 (2028). Thin coverage means the forecasts "the market" holds can move on one model, which raises the chance of large revisions in both directions and lowers the information content of consensus as a test of delivery.

How to weight and lean, line by line.

  • Revenue: lean up for Q4 2026 to Q2 2027 (my growth 21%, 23%, 11% against the quarters a year earlier); neutral for H2 2027 (5-10%); neutral for 2028 (the acquisition block does the work); down for 2029-30 against any extrapolation of 2026-27 rates.
  • Adjusted EBITA margin: lean up for Q1 2027 (an easy comparison with a 8.6% base), neutral for 2027 as a whole (11.0%), down against the 12% target for 2028 (11.3%), down for 2029-30 (about 11%).
  • EPS: lean up against the pre-Q3 consensus for 2026 (reported about SEK 2.40 against the FactSet median 2.30; Redeye has moved to 2.39), in line for 2027-28 (about SEK 2.8 and 3.3 against 2.71 and 3.23), down for 2029 (about SEK 3.6 against 4.18).
  • Cash flow: lean down against any model that converts net income to free cash flow at more than 80% in a growth year; the working-capital loop is the most reliable negative in the record (conversion 26%, 84%, 72% in the 2026 quarters).

Where I differ from the first set. The first set (C-69, C-74) said the cushion of a low base has been "partly used"; I go further: for 2026 it is fully used (the FY2026 figure is now a run-rate question, not a recovery question), and from Q2 2027 the comparisons are hard enough that the beat pattern is more likely to end than to continue, so the third set should not extrapolate the four-of-five record past mid-2027.

P-08

Below-operating-profit items, share count, and their effect on EPS, net income and free cash flow

C56

Question asked After assessing below-operating-profit cost items (e.g. tax, interest, one-offs, minority investments, etc.) and share buybacks or issuance in greater detail, how is it likely to shape earnings per share, net income and free cash flow over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement. The items between adjusted EBITA and EPS turn from a large drag in 2023-25 into a small net tailwind in 2026-28 and a neutral factor thereafter, but they also make reported EPS a noisy and systematically lower number than adjusted EBITA growth implies: the conversion of adjusted EBITA to reported net income is about 58% in 2026, rising to about 62-64% by 2029-30, against 69% in 2023, because amortisation of acquired intangibles (SEK 100m in 2026, rising about SEK 10m for every SEK 400m of acquisitions, to about SEK 140m by 2030) and a share count 8% higher after the May 2026 issue outweigh the gains from lower interest (SEK 151m in 2025 to about SEK 65-95m in 2027-30) and a lower tax rate (31.3% in 2024 to about 25-26%). The practical consequences for the model are four. First, reported EPS grows about two to three points a year slower than adjusted EBITA in 2027-30 (EPS about plus 17%, plus 18%, plus 9%, plus 11% against EBITA plus 21%, plus 15%, plus 7%, plus 9%). Second, free cash flow after leases but before acquisitions runs at about 70-100% of reported net income (lower in high-growth years, about 68% in 2027), and after the SEK 400m a year of acquisitions it is close to zero in 2027 and only SEK 200-450m in 2028-30, so the equity story is EPS growth and de-leveraging, not cash return. Third, the EPS line carries about plus or minus SEK 0.10-0.15 a year of noise from earn-out revaluations and items affecting comparability, which is 4-5% of EPS and enough to turn an in-line quarter into a reported beat or miss. Fourth, the 2023 reported EPS peak of SEK 4.17 is not regained until about 2030 in my base case, even though adjusted EBITA passes its 2023 level (SEK 1,234m) in 2028; the difference is 10% more shares and SEK 40m more amortisation.

Each item, with the history and the forecast.

  • Tax. Reported rates 22.0% (2022), 24.5% (2023), 31.3% (2024), 113% (2025, non-deductible restructuring in loss-making jurisdictions with no deferred-tax assets recognised), 26.7% for nine months 2026 (helped by deferred-tax-asset recognition against deferred-tax liabilities in the same jurisdiction), 29.6% in Q3 2026. The permanent driver is the profit shift to the United States (21% federal plus state, so about 25-26% all-in), where North America is now 48% of sales and a higher share of profit; the temporary driver is Swedish loss utilisation, which phases out. Pillar Two puts a 15% floor under subsidiaries but does not bind at these rates. Prysmian guides to about 27%, Lifco pays about 23%, Belden's normalised rate is 20%. My inputs: 26.5% (2026), 26% (2027-28), 25.5% (2029), 25% (2030); each point of tax rate is worth about SEK 0.04 of EPS in 2027.
  • Amortisation of acquired intangibles. SEK 123m (2024), SEK 108m (2025), about SEK 100m in 2026 (SEK 23-24m a quarter after older intangibles expired, plus Q4 additions). The 2026 acquisitions allocated SEK 77m to customer relations (out of SEK 551m), amortised over about five to ten years, adding about SEK 10-15m a year. My inputs: SEK 100m, 112m, 122m, 132m, 140m for 2026-30, consistent with SEK 400m a year of acquisitions. Brokers add this back to reach EBITA but not, in substance, to reach their EPS: Redeye's EPS is diluted EPS after amortisation, so the Consumer should compare my reported EPS with consensus, not a pre-amortisation figure.
  • Items affecting comparability. SEK -298m in 2025 (restructuring, facility, transition and legal); a net SEK +71m in Q3 2026 (the SEK 67m Clinton impairment reversal); small items in other 2026 quarters; about SEK +50m net for 2026. My base case carries zero from 2027, but the record (SEK 134m of non-recurring items in the twelve months to Q3 2025; SEK 298m in 2025; acquisition costs on every deal) says the expected value is negative by SEK 30-60m a year with a long negative tail in a downturn (a second European consolidation would cost SEK 100-150m). Reported EPS therefore has a negative skew relative to my base case of about SEK 0.10-0.20 a year.
  • Net financial items. Net interest SEK 200m (2024), 151m (2025), about SEK 105m (2026), falling to SEK 80-95m in 2027-28 and SEK 45-65m by 2030 as net debt excluding leases falls from SEK 1.3bn to about SEK 0.5bn; plus about SEK 20m a year of lease interest; plus revaluations of contingent consideration and acquisition options (SEK -28m in Q3 2026, SEK +20m in Q3 2025, SEK -10m for nine months 2026) that are negative when acquisitions outperform. Lifco and HMS Networks route put-and-call option revaluations through equity; Hexatronic routes contingent consideration through profit and loss, so its reported EPS is structurally noisier than those peers' for the same economics. My inputs for net financial items: SEK -132m, -105m, -95m, -80m, -65m.
  • Minority interests. SEK 31m on the balance sheet; the charge is about SEK 3-4m a year; negligible.
  • Share count. 205.1m shares at end-2025; 15,789,474 issued in May 2026 (about 7.7%); 924,800 C shares issued and repurchased in October 2026 to hedge incentives; LTIP 2026 adds about 0.41% at full allocation and outstanding rights total about 2.18% of shares (about 4.8m shares) over roughly three years. FactSet shows 221.7m diluted shares. My inputs: weighted diluted shares about 216m (2026), 223m (2027), 224m (2028), 225m (2029), 226m (2030), that is about 1m a year of non-cash dilution. No buybacks are in policy or in my base case; a further directed issue for a larger acquisition is the main risk to the count (about 30% probability over four years; a SEK 1bn issue at today's price would add about 24m shares, 11%).
  • Dividends. SEK 0.10 a share paid for 2020-22, zero since; policy is acquisitions first; consensus zero to 2028. My inputs: zero to 2029, SEK 0.50 in 2030 (a judgement of low confidence that an under-levered balance sheet and an EPS-weighted incentive plan produce a first payout by then; the alternative use is a larger acquisition, and I would put the two at even odds).
  • Currency. More than 90% of sales are outside Sweden; translation cut reported sales 5% in 2025 and 9 points in Q1 2026 and added 1 point in Q3 2026. A 10% stronger krona removes about 9% of reported revenue but only about SEK 22m of operating profit (GBP -21m, USD -8m, EUR +7m on 2025 figures), about 2% of EBITA, because production is local; the reported margin therefore rises when the krona strengthens, and EPS falls far less than revenue. I carry -2% translation in 2027 revenue (a Riksbank hike is "increasingly likely") and nothing thereafter; the Consumer should treat a 10% krona move as a plus or minus 9% revenue and plus or minus 3% EPS sensitivity, not a one-for-one.
  • Capitalised development. Small (SEK 9m capitalised against SEK 12m amortised in 2024); not a hidden drag or a hidden boost.

Free cash flow, built from the above. Operating cash flow after tax and interest about SEK 705m (2026, with nine months actual at SEK 438m), SEK 870m (2027), SEK 1,070m (2028), SEK 1,190m (2029), SEK 1,270m (2030); capex including intangibles SEK 140m, 330m, 320m, 260m, 270m (the subsea line of about SEK 300m and the South Carolina cable line fall in 2027-28); lease payments SEK 110-140m. Free cash flow after leases, before acquisitions: about SEK 455m, 420m, 620m, 790m, 860m, which is 90%, 68%, 84%, 98% and 96% of reported net income. After acquisitions of SEK 400m a year, free cash flow to equity is about SEK 20m (2027), 220m (2028), 390m (2029) and 460m (2030); 2026 is covered by the equity issue. The working-capital loop is the reason conversion is lowest in the highest-growth year.

Against the last four to six years. 2020-22: interest small (SEK 12-41m), tax 22-24%, amortisation rising with the acquisition spree, shares 188m to 203m, conversion of EBITA to net income 70-80%. 2023-25: interest SEK 150-200m, tax 24-31% then anomalous, amortisation SEK 108-123m, SEK 298m of one-offs, conversion collapsing to nil. 2026-30: interest halves again, tax normalises at 25-26%, amortisation grows slowly, shares 8-10% higher, conversion about 58-64%. The below-the-line picture is better than 2023-25 and worse than 2020-22, and it is the share count, not the cost lines, that makes the difference to EPS.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set's clean FY2026 EPS of SEK 2.25-2.40 is, on my build, about SEK 2.20 (reported about SEK 2.38 including the Q3 reversal); the difference is a Q4 seasonal step to about SEK 0.52 of reported EPS and a slightly higher Q4 tax rate. I also make explicit that the consensus EPS basis is post-amortisation reported EPS, which the first set did not.

P-09

The net likely impact on the main financial line items, against the last four to six years

C60

Question asked After assessing the other questions/items in the set in greater detail, what is the net likely impact to the main financial line items over the next 18 months to four years, versus the last four to six years?

Judgement. Netting the eight mechanisms above, the next four years look like a second, lower and broader cycle than 2021-25: revenue compounds at about 11% a year to 2030 (about 6% organic, 4% acquired, 1% from the subsea step and price), against about 26% a year in 2019-25 that was front-loaded into the FTTH boom and acquisitions; the adjusted EBITA margin rises from 8.4% to a plateau of about 11-11.5% in 2027-28 and drifts to about 11% by 2030, against a 2019-25 path that went from about 5% to 16.6% and back to 8.4%; EBITDA margin about 14%; reported EPS from SEK -0.07 (2025) to about SEK 2.4, 2.8, 3.3, 3.6 and 4.0 for 2026-30, so the 2023 peak of SEK 4.17 is regained only at the end of the horizon; free cash flow after leases of SEK 400-900m a year, mostly reinvested in acquisitions; net debt falling to near zero by 2030 absent a larger deal or a dividend. The change against the past is less in the level of growth than in its composition and its leverage: half the growth is bought at 12-15% margins, a quarter is services with variable costs, and only a quarter is manufacturing volume on the fixed base, so both the upside and the downside to margin are roughly half what they were in the last cycle (a 10% volume shock moves EBITA about 15-19% now against about 30-50% then). The two swing variables for 2027 are North American Fiber Solutions volume after the refill and the size of Data Center normalisation; for 2028-30 they are the arrival of competitive fibre capacity and the acquisition cadence. The distribution around the base case is skewed modestly down for 2027 (supply caps the upside, the US and hyperscaler channels open the downside) and more clearly down for 2029-30 against the single-broker consensus.

Where the net impact is most and least certain. Most certain: the 2026 outcome (nine months are in the accounts), the direction of interest and tax, the structural floor in Fiber Solutions margin (8-9% on flat volume against 5-6% in 2025, because the European cost base is lower), and the absence of a dividend before 2029. Least certain: the level at which Data Center settles after Q3 2026 (my 2027 organic growth of 15% has a plausible range of 5-25%), North American Fiber Solutions growth from Q2 2027 (my mid-to-high single digits has a range of -20% to +15%), the acquisition cadence and multiples (the base case needs about SEK 400m a year at 5-7x, which private-equity competition can disrupt), and the 2029-30 margin (the capacity cycle's timing could be a year earlier or later than my late-2027 assumption).

Skew of the cone. For 2027: base case EPS SEK 2.78 (about 55% weight), downside SEK 2.1-2.2 (about 25%: US refill ends in Q2 2027, Data Center grows 5%, krona 7% stronger), upside SEK 3.1-3.3 (about 20%: supply eases through validated suppliers, Data Center grows 25%, BEAD orders from mid-2027). Probability-weighted 2027 EPS about SEK 2.7, a little below the base case; the skew is modestly negative. For 2029: base case SEK 3.58 against the one-broker consensus of SEK 4.18; I put less than 25% probability on reaching the consensus figure.

Where I differ from the first set. The first set's 2027 EPS range of SEK 2.6-3.0 and EBITA of SEK 1.0-1.1bn stand; I narrow the base case to SEK 2.78 and SEK 1,057m and add the explicit finding that the 2023 EPS peak is not regained before about 2030, which the Consumer should weigh when reading a 13.8x multiple on 24-month-forward earnings.

P-10

The base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items

C60

Question asked Pulling together all the above answers, in all sets so far, what is your carefully considered base case for the key operational drivers and the key financial line items over the next 18 months to four years?

Judgement. My base case is a cyclical niche specialist in the second year of an upswing with a structurally lower cost base and a higher-margin mix than in its last cycle, growing revenue about 11% a year to 2030 (half organic, half acquired), holding an adjusted EBITA margin of about 11% from 2027, converting about 60% of adjusted EBITA to reported net income, and reinvesting most of its free cash flow in small acquisitions; reported EPS about SEK 2.38 (2026), 2.78 (2027), 3.29 (2028), 3.58 (2029) and 3.98 (2030). The next 18 months are the strongest part of the path (revenue growth of 21%, 23% and 11% in Q4 2026 to Q2 2027 against easy comparisons, margin 10-11%), then the rate of change falls sharply in H2 2027 (growth 5-10%) as US comparisons harden and Data Center's Q3 2026 timing effect reverses, and 2028-30 depend on the acquisition cadence, the subsea line and defence to offset the end of scarcity pricing. The operational drivers behind the numbers, and the ranges around them, are set out below so that the third set can test each one.

Key operational drivers, with the base-case assumption and the plausible range.

  • North American Fiber Solutions volume. Base: plus 12% in 2027 (plus 20-25% in Q1 against the cold-winter base, plus 5-10% from Q2), plus 8% in 2028 as BEAD orders ramp, plus 4-5% in 2029-30. Range: -20% to +20% for 2027. Watch: North American growth in Q2-Q3 2027 against the plus 44% and plus 51% comparisons; BEAD subgrantee orders; the South Carolina cable line commissioning (history says late).
  • Fiber Solutions Europe. Base: -3% in 2027, flat in 2028, plus 2% in 2029-30 as connection products offset passing. Range: -10% to +5%. Watch: UK and DACH build rates; any capacity exits by competitors; a second consolidation would be the signal of a downside scenario, not of the base case.
  • Data Center. Base: SEK 2.21bn (2026), 2.64bn (2027, organic plus 15% plus Superior annualisation, FX -3%), 2.90bn (2028, plus 10%), 3.11bn (2029, plus 7%), 3.29bn (2030, plus 6%); margin 16.2%, 15.5%, 15.0%, 14.0%, 13.5%. Range for 2027 organic growth: 5-25%. Watch: Q4 2026 sales against the first-half average of about SEK 486m a quarter (my SEK 550m assumes Superior and about 10% underlying growth); hyperscaler capex financing (CoreWeave was the first crack); the permanent head appointment.
  • Harsh Environment. Base: SEK 1.43bn (2026), 1.56bn (2027), 1.80bn (2028, with the subsea line from mid-year), 2.09bn (2029), 2.26bn (2030); margin 10.6%, 12.0%, 13.0%, 14.0%, 14.5%. The 15% target is not reached in the base case. Watch: dynamic-cable orders (oil and gas), JOWO's defence order book, the Hudiksvall permit.
  • Acquisitions. Base: SEK 400m a year from 2027 at about 6x EBITDA, adding about SEK 450m of annual revenue each year at a 12-12.5% margin, half-year effect in the year of purchase. Range: zero (if the multiple de-rates) to SEK 1bn plus a directed issue. Watch: deal announcements, multiples paid, private-equity competition for installer targets.
  • Price and input costs. Base: price plus 1-2 points of growth in 2026-27, flat in 2028, minus 1-2 points in 2029-30; gross margin 39-40% to 2027 then 38-39%. Watch: G.652.D spot fibre prices and lead times (a fall signals the end of scarcity about one quarter before it reaches the margin); any supplier allocation cut.
  • Supply constraint. Base: it caps 2027 organic growth at about 9% against demand indicators above 12% and adds about one point to working capital; it eases through 2028. Range: a lost quarter of shipments (10-15% probability) would cost about SEK 150-200m of revenue.
  • Currency. Base: -2% translation in 2027, nothing after. Range: a 10% krona move is plus or minus 9% revenue and plus or minus 3% EPS.
  • Working capital. Base: 24.0% of year-end sales in 2026-27, 23.5% in 2028, 23.0% in 2029-30 (FactSet definition: receivables plus inventories less payables). Watch: year-end inventory with supply constraints.
  • Capex. Base: SEK 140m (2026), 330m (2027), 320m (2028), 260m (2029), 270m (2030), with the subsea line (about SEK 300m) and the cable line in 2027-28.
  • Below the line. Tax 26.5% to 25%; net financial items SEK -132m to -65m; amortisation of acquired intangibles SEK 100m to 140m; items affecting comparability zero after 2026 (negative skew); shares 216m to 226m; no buybacks; dividend zero to 2029, SEK 0.50 in 2030.

Harsh Environment SEK 2bn at 15%: mine SEK 1.8bn at 13% (short on both). The LTIP 2026 vesting range (9.8-11.1% average margin 2026-28): mine averages 10.8%, inside the upper half.

The scenarios the third set should carry. Downside (about 25%): North American Fiber Solutions volume -20% from Q2 2027, Data Center plus 5%, krona 7% stronger, no new acquisitions: 2027 revenue about SEK 8.9bn, adjusted EBITA about SEK 860m (9.7%), EPS about SEK 2.1; 2028 revenue SEK 9.5bn, EBITA SEK 1.0bn, EPS about SEK 2.6; a second European consolidation (SEK 100-150m of one-offs) likely. Upside (about 20%): supply eases through validated suppliers, Data Center plus 25%, BEAD orders from mid-2027, North America plus 20%: 2027 revenue about SEK 10.1bn, EBITA about SEK 1,180m (11.7%), EPS about SEK 3.2; 2028 revenue SEK 11.5bn, margin 12%, EPS about SEK 3.9. The probability-weighted 2027 EPS is about SEK 2.7, marginally below the base case.

What would make me change the base case within six months. Q4 2026 Data Center sales below about SEK 500m or above SEK 600m; North American Fiber Solutions growth in Q4 2026 below 20% (the refill ending early) or Q1 2027 group organic growth below 15% against the easy base; a glass or connector allocation cut disclosed as lost revenue; a deal above EUR 50m or a directed issue (which would raise the acquired block and the share count together); a permanent CFO and Data Center head not named by February 2027 (which would widen the cone rather than move the base).
