The operating drivers behind the inputs

Revenue growth: 2026 plus 15.3% is nine months reported (SEK 6,441m) plus a Q4 of SEK 2,230m (plus 21% on SEK 1,848m: Data Center normalising toward about SEK 550m including Superior, Fiber Solutions plus 9%, Harsh Environment plus 30% with JOWO and submarine deliveries similar to Q3). 2027 plus 11.5% is about plus 9% organic (US absorption, Data Center plus 15%, Europe down), plus 4.5% acquired (annualisation of 2026 deals about 2 points, new deals about 2.5 points) and minus 2% translation on a stronger krona. 2028 plus 11.5% is about plus 6% organic (US BEAD ramp, Data Center plus 10%, subsea start), plus 5.5% acquired. 2029-30 plus 10% and plus 8% are plus 3.5-4% organic (competitive capacity arriving, data-centre optical demand growth slowing from 2028) plus 4.5-6% acquired and the subsea step. The supply constraint on glass and connectors caps 2027 organic growth below the demand indicators and eases through 2028.

EBITDA margin: adjusted EBITA margin 10.1%, 10.9%, 11.3%, 10.9%, 11.0% plus depreciation of 3.3%, 3.2%, 3.1%, 3.0%, 2.9% of sales. The EBITA path is driven by Fiber Solutions absorption (about plus 70 basis points to group in 2027), the SEK 120m cost programme already in the 2026 base, scale on operating costs (25.0% of sales in Q3 2026 to about 24% by 2028), depreciation dilution (capex below depreciation through 2026, reversing in 2028-29 with the subsea and South Carolina lines), and dilution from services and acquired units at 12-15% margins; price is pass-through with a one-quarter lag and contributes nothing to margin in the base case. Gross margin held at 39-40% to 2027 and 38-39% after.

Depreciation and amortisation: SEK 290m in 2025 (FactSet total SEK 402m less about SEK 108m of amortisation of acquired intangibles), including depreciation of right-of-use assets; flat in 2026 (capex SEK 140m against depreciation SEK 290m), then rising with the SEK 300m subsea line, the cable line and acquired right-of-use assets. Amortisation of acquired intangibles, which the model leaves out of adjusted earnings, is about SEK 100m (2026), 112m, 122m, 132m, 140m; it is in my EPS cross-check because consensus EPS is after it.

Net financial items: net interest SEK 81m for nine months 2026 (SEK 26m in Q3) plus lease interest of about SEK 20m a year plus an allowance of about SEK 10m a year for negative revaluations of contingent consideration (SEK -28m in Q3 2026); falling as net debt excluding leases goes from SEK 1,305m (Q3 2026) to about SEK 1.1bn (end-2026 and end-2027), 0.9bn (2028), 0.5bn (2029) and 0.1bn (2030) after SEK 400m a year of acquisitions.

Tax rate: 26.7% for nine months 2026 (helped by deferred-tax-asset recognition), 29.6% in Q3; the US profit mix (North America 48% of sales) lowers the normal rate toward 25% over the horizon; 31.3% in 2024 and the anomalous 2025 rate are not representative.

Dividend: zero for 2023-25 and in policy (acquisitions first); zero through 2029; SEK 0.50 in 2030 as a low-confidence judgement that an under-levered balance sheet produces a first payout; the alternative use is a larger acquisition.

Share count: the 15,789,474 shares issued at SEK 38 in May 2026 (SEK 600m gross, about SEK 580m net) are in Part 2 as new shares issued for cash; the Part 1 line carries only incentive-programme dilution (LTIP 2026 about 0.41%; about 2.18% including existing rights, roughly 4.8m shares over three years), about 1m shares a year.

Capital spending: SEK 91m for nine months 2026 (1.5% of Q3 sales) plus a Q4 of about SEK 50m; 2027-28 include the Hudiksvall subsea line (Redeye estimate about SEK 300m; the company has not disclosed the amount) and the South Carolina fibre-cable line; 2029-30 revert to about 2-2.5% of sales. Consensus capex SEK 163m, 264m, 279m for 2026-28.

Working capital: 23.4% of trailing sales at end-2025 and 26.1% at Q2 2026 (FactSet definition: receivables plus inventories less payables); the year-end is the seasonal low; supply constraints push inventory up in 2026-27; services revenue carries less inventory, so the ratio eases to 23% by 2029. Each SEK 1bn of revenue growth absorbs about SEK 230-260m.

Lease payments: lease liabilities of about SEK 475m (FactSet lease-inclusive net debt SEK 1,946m at Q2 2026 against the company's SEK 1,470m); payments about SEK 110m a year, rising with acquired service businesses.

Acquisitions: SEK 527m of cash outflows for business combinations in nine months 2026 (JOWO, Superior and earn-outs on KNET, DCS and Qubix) plus about SEK 30m in Q4; SEK 400m a year from 2027, including earn-out settlements for JOWO (maximum EUR 7.6m, 2026-30 earnings) and Superior (maximum USD 3m, 2026-29 earnings) that fall in 2029-31.

The company's guidance, and how your inputs compare with it

The company gives no numeric annual guidance. Qualitative cues for Q4 2026 (22 October 2026): Data Center to normalise toward the first-half pattern after favourable seasonality and project timing in Q3; Q4 and Q1 seasonally lower than Q2 and Q3; submarine deliveries similar to Q3; continued US growth; European FTTH and transport subdued; glass fibre and selected connectors beginning to restrict growth with longer lead times. My Q4 2026 (sales SEK 2,230m, Data Center about SEK 550m, adjusted EBITA margin 10.1%) follows these cues and sits slightly above a mechanical reading of them, because the cues have been conservative for a year (the July 2026 warning of Q3 margin pressure preceded a margin of 11.3%).

2028 targets (set September 2025 and May 2026, acquisitions included): group net sales SEK 11bn and an EBITA margin of about 12%; Fiber Solutions SEK 6bn at 10%; Data Center SEK 3bn at 15%; Harsh Environment SEK 2bn at 15%. My 2028: group SEK 10.78bn at 11.3%; Data Center SEK 2.90bn at 15.0% (met); Fiber Solutions SEK 5.41bn at 9.8% plus part of the unannounced-acquisition block (about SEK 0.3-0.4bn short); Harsh Environment SEK 1.80bn at 13.0% (short on both). The LTIP 2026 vests on an average EBITA margin of 9.8% to 11.1% over 2026-28; my average is 10.8%. Acquisition framework: three to six deals a year, under EUR 50m, at 5-7x EBITDA; my SEK 400m a year is consistent with it. Capex guideline (2025 call) of 3-4% of sales against an actual run-rate of 1.5-3%; my 2027-28 figures (about 3% of sales) reflect the subsea project rather than a return to the guideline.

The bridge from the company's own reported figures to the basis consensus uses

Revenue: the company reported SEK 7,519m for FY2025; FactSet fundamentals show SEK 7,245m and the brokers'-basis actual SEK 7,543m. My growth rates are on the company's figures; applied to a FactSet base they give revenue about 3.6% lower (fundamentals) or 0.3% higher (brokers' basis) than my SEK figures in every year, which does not change any ratio.

Operating profit: the company, Redeye and the Modular Finance consensus use adjusted EBITA (before items affecting comparability and before amortisation of acquired intangibles): SEK 632m for FY2025. Consensus EBITDA (FactSet median SEK 1,147m for 2026, 13.5%) is adjusted EBITA plus depreciation including right-of-use depreciation, the same construction as my EBITDA margin input (13.4%); FactSet's FY2025 brokers'-basis EBITDA of SEK 804m and FactSet operating EBITDA of SEK 476m are on other bases and should not be used as the base for growth to my figures (my FY2025 EBITDA on the model's basis is SEK 922m). FactSet consensus EBIT (SEK 734m for 2026) is after amortisation of acquired intangibles (consensus EBITDA less consensus D&A of SEK 297m gives SEK 850m; the difference of about SEK 116m is that amortisation).

EPS: consensus "adjusted" EPS on FactSet (SEK 2.30, 2.71, 3.23, 4.18 for 2026-29; Redeye post-Q3 SEK 2.39 for 2026) is in substance reported diluted EPS after amortisation of acquired intangibles and including items affecting comparability (Redeye's SEK 2.39 includes the SEK 67m Clinton impairment reversal, about SEK 0.23 after tax; FactSet's FY2025 brokers'-basis EPS of SEK -0.33 is close to the reported SEK -0.07). My cross-check EPS is on that basis. The model's adjusted EPS, which leaves out amortisation of acquired intangibles, will therefore sit about SEK 0.35-0.45 above my cross-check in every year; the gap is amortisation of acquired intangibles after tax (SEK 100-140m pre-tax over 216-226m shares), plus SEK 0.17 of net one-offs after tax in 2026 only. This is the main reason the model's EPS and my cross-check will differ, and it is a basis difference, not a forecast difference.

Free cash flow: FactSet's consensus free cash flow (SEK 70m for 2026) appears to be after acquisitions; FactSet's historical free cash flow is operating cash flow less fixed-asset capex only. My cross-check free cash flow is operating cash flow after tax and interest, less capital spending including intangibles, less lease payments, before acquisitions and before equity issuance.

Net debt: the company's net debt excludes lease liabilities (SEK 1,305m at Q3 2026; adjusted leverage 1.3x); FactSet's includes them (SEK 1,946m at Q2 2026). Covenants are on the company's basis.

Free cash flow is operating cash flow after tax and interest (about SEK 705m, 870m, 1,070m, 1,190m, 1,270m) less capital spending including intangibles and less lease payments, before acquisitions of SEK 560m, 400m, 400m, 400m, 400m and before the SEK 580m equity issue in 2026. The model's adjusted EPS will be about SEK 0.35-0.45 above my figures each year because it excludes amortisation of acquired intangibles; that difference is expected and should not be read as a disagreement.

Appendix Research requests the Investment Analyst raised for Layer 3 and Layer 4

Research requests for the next set

Company: Hexatronic Group AB (HTRO-SE; Nasdaq Stockholm), a Swedish maker of passive fibre-optic infrastructure reporting three segments: Fiber Solutions (microducts, ducts, air-blown fibre cables, connectivity and submarine fibre cable for FTTH, transport and middle-mile networks in Europe, North America and Asia-Pacific; about 57% of Q3 2026 sales), Data Center (high-density cabling and connectivity plus design, installation and integration services for hyperscale, colocation and enterprise data centres, mainly in the United States; about 28%) and Harsh Environment (dynamic cables, specialist connectors and fibre sensing for defence, offshore energy and marine; about 16%); North America was 48% of Q3 2026 sales, Europe 43%, Asia-Pacific 9%.

Request: T-D1

Peers: Dura-Line (Orbia Connectivity Solutions; the US duct market leader at about 65% share, which went through the same 2023-25 destock and 2026 recovery at higher margins); Clearfield (US fibre products through distributors; the clearest destock-and-recovery precedent, with gross margin from 35.7% to 13.7% and back to 31.8%); Prysmian (Digital Solutions; integrated fibre maker that repriced and reallocated capacity to hyperscalers, margin 16.8% to 23.8% in a year); Corning (Optical Communications; Hexatronic's glass supplier and data-centre competitor, with multi-year hyperscaler contracts); Emtelle (private, UK; the closest European blown-fibre system competitor); Nexans (Scandinavian telecom cable competitor and a cable-cycle precedent); NKT (subsea partner under a framework to 2032, and a high-voltage overcapacity precedent); Amphenol (owner of CommScope's connectivity business since 2026; data-centre connectivity scale competitor); Dycom Industries (US fibre and data-centre installation contractor; the model for variable-cost services); HMS Networks and Lifco (Nordic serial acquirers of small bolt-ons, for the record of buying at 5-8x EBITDA and lifting margins); Belden (connectivity solutions mix shift); Wize Solutions (private-equity installer roll-up competing for the same service targets). Operating conditions: the US FTTH and middle-mile rebuild after an 18-to-24-month destock, with private-equity-backed tier-2 and tier-3 builders restarting and BEAD subgrantee contracts signed in 2026 for construction from 2027 under Build America Buy America rules; the AI data-centre build with hyperscalers prioritising availability over price, and the risk of a financing-led pause; the 2025-26 glass-fibre and preform shortage (spot G.652.D fibre up several-fold since July 2025) with new preform and fibre capacity announced for 2027-28 (Corning plus 50%, Prysmian EUR 1.25bn in US fibre, Chinese additions); the maturity of European FTTH, with operators moving from passing homes to connecting them and microduct overcapacity; rising European defence budgets and deferred offshore oil-and-gas projects; a strengthening Swedish krona and rising rates; integration of founder-led service acquisitions kept as independent units. Inputs: Hexatronic's management targets for 2028 (set September 2025 and May 2026, acquisitions included): group net sales SEK 11bn and an EBITA margin of about 12%; Fiber Solutions SEK 6bn at 10%; Data Center SEK 3bn at 15%; Harsh Environment SEK 2bn at 15%; the LTIP 2026 vesting range of a 9.8% to 11.1% average EBITA margin over 2026-28 (EGM notice, 22 July 2026). The expected changes to test: (1) Fiber Solutions sustaining a margin near 10% on North American volume after a recovery from 5.2% (Q4 2025) to 9.4% (Q3 2026), while Fiber Solutions Europe keeps falling (minus 31%, 10% and 9% in Q1-Q3 2026): how often have duct and cable makers held a recovered margin once the post-destock refill ended and comparisons hardened, and what distinguished those that did? (2) Data Center growing from about SEK 1.4bn (2025) to SEK 3bn (2028) at a 15% margin while services (variable labour, about 12-13% standalone margins at acquired units such as Superior Fiber & Data Services) become the larger part: precedents of product companies scaling installation services with acquisitions, and whether margins held. (3) Harsh Environment moving from an 11% margin to 15% with dynamic cables depressed by oil-and-gas deferrals and defence connectors (JOWO, acquired April 2026) growing: precedents of harsh-environment connector and cable businesses lifting margins by four points in three years. (4) The company's record of delivery: the SEK 110m programme delivered as SEK 120m ahead of schedule (Q1 2026); four consecutive quarters at or above the company-commissioned consensus on profit (Q4 2025 to Q3 2026) after misses and a profit warning in 2024-25; capacity commissioning slips at Clinton (2022), South Carolina (2023), Ogden (2024) and the South Carolina cable line (deferred to 2027); the Clinton downsizing decided in Q4 2025 and reversed in Q3 2026. (5) Strengths demonstrably held (installation-saving air-blown systems with installer training and certification, local BABA-compliant duct plants in the US, defence certifications, the NKT subsea framework to 2032) against strengths that would need to be acquired (secured glass supply, high-density data-centre intellectual property, scale against Corning, Amphenol and Prysmian, a permanent CFO and a permanent Data Center head, both vacant at October 2026). (6) The acquisition engine: three to six founder-led deals a year under EUR 50m at 5-7x EBITDA, with the deputy chief executive who built the programme leaving in 2026 and private-equity roll-ups bidding for larger targets: how often have Nordic and sector serial acquirers kept both the cadence and the multiples through a cycle, and what happened when they could not. Focus: Whether a non-integrated, mid-sized passive-fibre company has ever converted a post-destock volume recovery plus an acquisition-led pivot into services into a durable margin at or above 11-12%, from a starting point like Hexatronic's (European plants under-absorbed, US plants filling, glass supply constrained), and how long such a change took where it succeeded; secondarily, whether the Harsh Environment margin step to 15% has a precedent in comparable connector and cable businesses.

Request: T-D2

Peers: Dura-Line (Orbia; US duct absorption cycle, the dispersion of its margins through 2021-26); Clearfield (the widest recorded margin dispersion through the destock and recovery); Prysmian (Digital Solutions; how concentrated its margin recovery was on hyperscaler repricing); Corning (Optical Communications; the dispersion of its optical revenue through the 2023-24 carrier inventory correction and 2025-26 AI build); Nexans and NKT (the 2016-20 high-voltage overcapacity and its reversal, for how capacity cycles widened outcome ranges); Emtelle (private; European blown-fibre pricing behaviour); Amphenol (CommScope connectivity; forecast dispersion around hyperscaler timing); Dycom Industries and MasTec (installation contractors; the dispersion of data-centre and fibre revenue around customer timing); HMS Networks (a Nordic serial acquirer; how acquisitions widened or narrowed the range of outcomes); Fujikura and Sterlite Technologies (data-centre cabling; capacity additions and pricing). Operating conditions: the US fibre rebuild after a destock, with the question of how much of a recovery year is refill versus use; the AI data-centre build and the risk of a hyperscaler capex pause or financing event (the first credit crack in the AI build appeared in October 2026); the glass-fibre shortage and the arrival of new preform and fibre capacity in 2027-28; European FTTH maturity and microduct overcapacity; defence budget growth with lumpy procurement timing; offshore oil-and-gas deferrals; translation of a largely non-Swedish business into a strengthening krona; a thin sell-side (one visible broker on FactSet; four to five contributors to the company-commissioned consensus). Inputs: the drivers that separate the next 18 months to four years from 2020-25 and that the report should test for concentration and dispersion: (1) North American Fiber Solutions volume, which grew 44% in Q2 2026 and 51% in Q3 2026 on a direct-sales model with frame agreements, against hard comparisons from Q2 2027: how dispersed were year-two outcomes for duct and cable makers after comparable post-destock surges, and how often did forecasts that rested on sustained US volume prove right? (2) Data Center organic growth (37% in 2025; 20%, 27% and 44% in Q1-Q3 2026, with management saying Q3 benefited from seasonality and project timing and that Q4 will normalise toward the first-half pattern): the dispersion of quarterly data-centre cabling and installation revenue around project timing at peers and contractors, and the frequency of pauses. (3) Fiber Solutions Europe (minus 9% to minus 31% a quarter in 2026) and whether a stabilisation is a plausible driver or an outlier. (4) The supply constraint: glass fibre and selected connectors "beginning to restrict growth" (22 October 2026); how often supply-capped recoveries at non-integrated cable makers ended in lost orders or in deferred revenue, and how wide the range of outcomes was. (5) The acquisition cadence (SEK 551m of acquisitions in 2026 including contingent consideration; three to six deals a year targeted) as a driver that is diffuse in count but concentrated in one decision-maker, with the M&A architect departed. (6) Operating leverage: Fiber Solutions incremental and decremental margins on volume in 2023-26; how often a company with this leverage met forecasts that rested on one or two volume drivers. (7) The historical dispersion of Hexatronic's own outcomes against forecasts (FY2026 EPS consensus from SEK 5.05 to a low of SEK 1.76 and back above SEK 2.3; the September 2025 rebasing of targets) and against peers' dispersion. (8) Events that widened or narrowed the cone at peers: capacity commissioning, customer concentration (one material Communication Zone project in Q3 2026), earn-out revaluations, currency. Focus: How concentrated the next 18 months' outcome is on two external channels (US carrier spending and hyperscaler spending) and one supply constraint, how wide the historical dispersion of outcomes has been for comparable companies in the second year after a destock recovery, and how often forecasts resting on one or two such drivers were met; secondarily, which observable indicators at peers and in the supply chain narrowed the cone fastest in past cycles.

Request: T-I1

Peers: Corning (Optical Communications; Q3 2026 results and guidance in late October 2026 are the most direct read-across for data-centre and carrier fibre demand); Prysmian (Q3 2026 results due 29 October 2026; Digital Solutions margins, US capacity and hyperscaler agreements); Dura-Line (Orbia; US conduit volumes and pricing); Clearfield (fiscal 2026 outlook cut for rural delays; backlog down 34% sequentially to June 2026); Nexans (9M 2026 reported 22 October 2026); NKT (subsea framework partner); Amphenol (CommScope connectivity; data-centre demand commentary); Dycom Industries (fiscal 2027 outlook for fibre and data-centre installation); Nokia (optical component scarcity and AI networking demand); HMS Networks (Nordic small-cap with a record of guiding and of consensus behaviour around results); Fujikura and Sterlite Technologies (data-centre cabling demand and capacity). Operating conditions: the US fibre rebuild and the timing of BEAD orders in 2027; the AI data-centre build with hyperscaler capex plans for 2027 and the risk of a financing-led pause; the glass-fibre shortage and lead-time extensions through 2026-27 with new capacity from 2027-28; European FTTH maturity; European defence procurement and deferred offshore oil-and-gas orders; a strengthening Swedish krona (a Riksbank rate rise expected in November 2026); seasonally weaker Q4 and Q1 quarters at the company; a thin sell-side with a company-commissioned consensus published before each report. Inputs: Hexatronic gives no numeric annual guidance; its stated cues for Q4 2026 (22 October 2026) are Data Center normalising toward the first-half pattern after favourable seasonality and project timing in Q3, Q4 and Q1 seasonally lower than Q2 and Q3, submarine deliveries similar to Q3, continued US growth, European FTTH and transport subdued, and glass fibre and selected connectors beginning to restrict growth; its 2028 targets are SEK 11bn of sales and an EBITA margin of about 12% including acquisitions, held unchanged through three consecutive beats. The record to test: Q3 2026 beat the company-commissioned consensus by about 11% on sales (SEK 2,498m against SEK 2,256m), 21% on adjusted EBITA (SEK 281m against SEK 233m) and 37% on diluted EPS (SEK 0.81 against SEK 0.59, including a SEK 67m impairment reversal); beats in Q4 2025 and Q2 2026 on sales and profit; a sales miss but profit beat in Q1 2026 (cold winter); misses and a profit warning in 2024-25 under the prior chief executive; a July 2026 warning of Q3 margin pressure that did not materialise. The dynamics to test: (1) what Corning's, Prysmian's, Dura-Line's, Clearfield's and Dycom's latest results and outlooks imply for Hexatronic's Q4 2026 and H1 2027 in US fibre and data-centre installation; (2) where consensus for Q4 2026 and FY2027 sits against the company's Q3 2026 run-rate (SEK 2,498m of quarterly sales, 11.3% margin) and against its Q4 cues; (3) the company's record of guiding conservatively since the September 2025 rebasing; (4) how the easy Q1 2027 comparison (Q1 2026 sales SEK 1,698m, adjusted EBITA SEK 146m, organic growth minus 2%) and the hard Q2-Q3 2027 comparisons (North American Fiber Solutions plus 44% and plus 51%) shape the likelihood of beats and misses by quarter; (5) what a glass or connector allocation cut, a hyperscaler capex pause, a US refill ending or a 7-10% stronger krona would each do to the next four reports; (6) whether the 2028 targets or the segment targets are likely to be raised, held or cut at a capital markets day or results in the next 18 months, and what would drive each; (7) how the thin coverage (one visible broker; four to five contributors) affects the size and speed of revisions. Focus: Whether the Q3 2026 beat and the easy Q1 2027 comparison make further beats and upward revisions likely through the Q1 2027 report, and whether the hardening of comparisons from the Q2 2027 report, Data Center normalisation and the supply constraint make a miss or a downward revision likely in H2 2027; secondarily, whether the 2028 targets are more likely to be raised (Data Center) or quietly missed (Harsh Environment, group margin).

Question: For Hexatronic Group AB (HTRO-SE), how much of the 2026 surge in US fibre-to-the-home, transport and middle-mile demand (North American Fiber Solutions sales up 44% in Q2 2026 and 51% in Q3 2026; North America 48% of group sales) is end-use, how much is refilling of depleted channel and customer inventory after the 2023-25 destock, and how much is price: what do distributor and carrier channel checks (Graybar, Wesco, Anixter, Clearfield's commentary on community broadband, Dura-Line and Orbia's conduit volumes, Corning's carrier segment, Calix and Adtran) show about customer inventory levels and ordering patterns in H2 2026; what are private-equity-backed tier-2 and tier-3 builders (for example Novos Fiber, Ting, Brightspeed, Metronet, Lumos and GoNetspeed) saying about 2027 build plans and funding at higher interest rates; by state, when are BEAD subgrantee contracts expected to convert into materials orders for ducts and cable, what fibre share of BEAD funds has been awarded, and what premium if any do Build America Buy America-compliant duct and cable makers command; and what did the comparable year-two period after the 2001 and 2022-23 fibre overshoots look like for duct and cable volumes?

Request: OWN-3

Question: For Hexatronic Group AB (HTRO-SE), how have its share price and its forward price-to-earnings multiple moved relative to the optical and fibre cohort (Corning, Prysmian, Nokia, Clearfield, Fujikura, Amphenol, Dura-Line's parent Orbia), relative to European and Swedish small-cap indices and relative to Nordic hardware peers (HMS Networks, Kitron, Invisio) on a rolling six-month basis from 2022 to October 2026; which company-specific events (quarterly reports, the September 2025 rebasing of targets, the May 2026 directed share issue, the Clinton reversal, acquisitions, leadership changes) moved the shares idiosyncratically and by how much against the cohort on the day and over the following month; how did the shares behave around cohort events (Corning and Prysmian results, hyperscaler capex announcements, the October 2026 "AI monetisation anxiety" sell-off in optical stocks, Riksbank decisions); what is the shareholder register (Accendo Capital about 5%, institutions in the May 2026 issue, insider holdings and dealings after the 90-day lock-up, short interest), and how does the stock's liquidity (about USD 7m a day) and thin coverage affect its co-movement with the sector?

Request: OWN-4

Question: For Hexatronic Group AB (HTRO-SE), which operating indicators does the company disclose each quarter (segment sales, organic growth and adjusted EBITA by segment; geographic split; an order book of about four months of sales; cash conversion; adjusted leverage) and which it does not (order intake, book-to-bill, backlog ageing, price against volume, Fiber Solutions order book, project-level submarine timing, the size of price increases, services renewal rates), compared with the disclosure of Dura-Line (Orbia), Clearfield, Corning, Prysmian, Nexans, NKT, HMS Networks and Dycom Industries; which external data series (CRU or other G.652.D bare-fibre spot price indices, fibre and connector lead times, US fibre construction and BEAD award trackers, FTTH Council Europe build data, hyperscaler capex guidance, Swedish krona rates, Corning and Prysmian quarterly commentary) have historically led Hexatronic's reported organic growth and gross margin, and by how many months; and which of the company's own qualitative cues at past quarterly reports since 2023 proved predictive of the following one or two quarters and which did not, so that an investor can monitor operating momentum over the next six months?

Request: OWN-5

Question: For Hexatronic Group AB (HTRO-SE), which disclosed on 22 October 2026 that glass fibre and selected connectors were beginning to restrict its growth with rising backlogs and lead-time extensions, what evidence exists from H2 2026 that non-integrated fibre-cable and connectivity makers in Europe and the United States (Hexatronic, Emtelle, Clearfield, Belden, Huber+Suhner, HFCL, Sterlite Technologies, Nexans' telecom business) have lost orders, deferred deliveries, paid spot premiums or built inventory because of glass or connector allocation by Corning, YOFC, Fujikura, Furukawa, Prysmian and other preform owners; how long new-supplier validation for bare fibre and connectors typically takes in telecom and data-centre networks; what the announced preform and fibre capacity additions (Corning plus 50%, Prysmian EUR 1.25bn in the US, Sterlite plus 50%, HFCL to 63m fibre-km by October 2028, about 230m fibre-km in China by end-2027) and current spot prices and lead times imply for when availability normalises and prices fall; and how quickly, in past easing cycles (2018-21 in China; 2023-24 in the US), input-cost declines were passed back to customers through indexed contracts and what that did to assemblers' gross margins quarter by quarter?

Request: OWN-6

Question: For Hexatronic Group AB (HTRO-SE), whose Harsh Environment segment (SEK 393m of Q3 2026 sales, 6% organic growth, an adjusted EBITA margin of 11.0% against a 2028 target of SEK 2bn at 15%) combines defence and marine connectors (including JOWO Systemtechnik, acquired April 2026), dynamic cables for offshore oil and gas (Rochester Cable, Fibron) and fibre sensing (Proximion), plus the submarine fibre-cable business in Hudiksvall whose capacity is to more than double for NKT by 2028, what do order books, procurement calendars and budget decisions in European defence (Germany's budget committee, NATO spending plans, drone and tactical fibre demand), offshore oil-and-gas drilling and subsea intervention (Transocean, Oceaneering, TechnipFMC, Subsea7 commentary on 2027 activity), and submarine cable security and route diversity (NKT, Nexans, Prysmian, Hellenic Cables, SubCom) imply for the segment's volume and mix in 2027-28; what margins do comparable harsh-environment connector and dynamic-cable businesses (Amphenol Harsh Environment Solutions at about 30%, TE Connectivity, Radiall, Bel Fuse, Fischer Connectors, Siemens Energy and Nexans subsea) earn and why Hexatronic's are lower; and what is the status and expected timing of the Hudiksvall environmental permit revision and the new subsea line's commissioning?

Layer 3 and Layer 4

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months
and Layer 4: Assessing the extent to which our assessment of the foundational components and financial forecasts is reflected or not in current company guidance, sell-side estimates and stock market narrative, primarily for the next 18 months and secondarily for 18 months to 3 years out

10 questions in 4 groups · 13,699 words · written 25 October 2026, 04:51 to 05:11 UK, Fable, main login

What the Investment Analyst was asked

The objective is to produce a robustly tested assessment of the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months, based on the first and second sets' answers.

The objective is to produce that judgement, based on the first and second sets' answers and your answers to the third set, for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo, so that Richard, who reads the memo, can form his judgement on the investment case.

What the scores mean in this layer

Finish each answer and each judgement on a group or set with a score from 1 to 100; a higher number is more positive for an investment case in the company.

For the third set's questions, the score says how positive the thing the question asks about is for the predictability of the company's financial forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case in the company: 100 the most positive, 1 the most negative, 50 neither.

For the fourth set's questions, the score says how favourable for an investment case in the company the gap is between our assessment and what current guidance, sell-side estimates and the stock market narrative already reflect: 100 the most favourable, 1 the least, 50 neither.

The Investment Analyst's opening notes for this layer

The Investment Analyst's title
Hexatronic (HTRO-SE): Third and fourth sets of questions, the predictability of the forecasts and their reflection in guidance, consensus and narrative

Prepared 25 October 2026 for the Investment Analyst who writes the investment memo for Richard.

Reading notes for the Consumer (read before the answers)

  • Bases. All company figures are on the company's own basis (FY2025 revenue SEK 7,519m; adjusted EBITA before items affecting comparability and before amortisation of acquired intangibles). "Consensus EPS" is in substance reported diluted EPS after amortisation of acquired intangibles (the second set established this; Redeye's post-Q3 FY2026 EPS of SEK 2.39 includes the SEK 67m Clinton impairment reversal). The Financial Analyst's model reports an adjusted EPS that leaves out amortisation of acquired intangibles, so it sits SEK 0.35-0.45 above the consensus basis every year by construction. The second set's cross-check EPS (SEK 2.38, 2.78, 3.29, 3.58, 3.98 for 2026-30) is the figure to compare with consensus, not the model's (SEK 2.62, 3.34, 3.91, 4.28, 4.71).
  • The model summary carries two artefacts the memo must not inherit. (1) FY2026 revenue of SEK 8,986.5m and "+24.0%" growth: the Part 3 quarterly growth inputs were applied to FactSet's quarterly revenue history, which is 5-8% above the company's own Q3 and Q4 2025 figures, so every year's revenue in the model is about 3.6% above the second set's company-basis figures and EBITA is SEK 38-67m too high. (2) The "against consensus" table compares pre-amortisation EBITA and EPS with post-amortisation consensus, so "+14.1% EPS against consensus for FY2026" is in truth +3.5% (SEK 2.38 against SEK 2.30), and against Redeye's post-Q3 SEK 2.39 it is nil. D-01 sets out the full reconciliation of the flagged gaps.
  • Consensus after Q3 2026. FactSet's weekly means (FY2026 SEK 2.26; FY2027 SEK 2.67; FY2028 SEK 3.07, as of 16 October) are pre-Q3. Redeye (23 October 2026) now has SEK 2.39, 2.85 and 3.36 for 2026-28, revenue SEK 9,631m for 2027 and SEK 10,213m for 2028, a Q4 2026 of SEK 2,254m of sales at a 9.6% EBITA margin with Data Center at SEK 582m, a Q1 2027 of SEK 2,038m and SEK 217m of EBITA, a Q2 2027 of SEK 2,563m, and a base case of SEK 46 (from SEK 44). The company-commissioned consensus has four contributors; FactSet names one. When FactSet absorbs Redeye's update, the dashboard will register a mechanical +5% to +9% EPS revision that reflects the Q3 print, not new information.
  • Earnings events in the next 18 months: Q4 2026 report on 5 February 2027; Q1 2027 in late April 2027; Q2 2027 in mid-July 2027; Q3 2027 in late October 2027; Q4 2027 in early February 2028; Q1 2028 in late April 2028. External read-across in the next two weeks: Corning Q3 (late October), Prysmian Q3 (29 October), the Riksbank decision (November).
  • Where I disagree with the earlier sets (each is argued inside the answer concerned): US duct pricing is under pressure from domestic overcapacity in 2026 (Orbia, Q2 and Q3 2026), so the first set's reading of benign US pricing applies to glass-linked cable surcharges, not to Hexatronic's main US product; Data Center services were 39% of nine-month 2026 segment sales (SEK 656m), not about half, so the segment's operating leverage is higher both ways than the second set assumed; the glass shortage is concentrated in bend-insensitive G.657.A2 data-centre fibre (spot about USD 35 per fibre-km) and MPO connectors, while standard G.652.D telecom fibre has already fallen from USD 17-18 to USD 11-13 per fibre-km, so the constraint binds Data Center products sooner and Fiber Solutions cable costs may ease earlier than the first set assumed; the Hudiksvall permit has slipped to late 2026 and is not yet granted; and the second set's free-cash-flow cross-check cannot be reproduced from its own working-capital input, which I change (section on inputs).
  • Scores are 1 to 100. Third set: how positive the item is for the predictability of the forecasts over the next 18 months, and so for an investment case. Fourth set: how favourable for an investment case the gap is between our assessment and what guidance, consensus and the narrative already reflect.

Third set: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Layer 3: Assessing the predictability of the financial forecasts for the next 18 months

Risks

C527 questions
D-0170D-0260D-0348D-0444D-0552D-0655D-0755

Group judgement written last by the Investment Analyst; shown first here

The seven answers point the same way: the forecasts for the next 18 months are more predictable than this company's history suggests and less predictable than a typical Nordic industrial of its size, and the predictability is front-loaded. The earlier sets and the model are congruent (D-01), the near-term expected change rests on strengths the business has demonstrated (D-02), and the next two reports are bounded by an easy comparison base, pre-announced normalisation, a four-month order book and a company that has under-promised for a year; through the Q1 2027 report in late April the base case is likely to be met or beaten and the cone is narrow. From Q2 2027 the case rests on two correlated external channels, US private fibre spending and hyperscaler spending, that together account for about three quarters of the organic difference between the forecasts and the past (D-03), that the company does not disclose in advance (D-05), and that transmit to EPS with 30-40% leverage in Fiber Solutions and ±SEK 0.10-0.20 of reported-EPS noise (D-06); the cone on 2027 EPS is about -25% to +18% around SEK 2.78 with a modestly negative skew, and wider on 2028 (D-04). The multiple is company-driven and the share price is thin and sentiment-exposed, so delivery is rewarded and a stumble is punished before it reaches the accounts (D-07). Three steers for the memo. First, present the forecasts as two periods: a high-confidence six months (two reports, both likely beats against the four-contributor consensus) and a lower-confidence twelve months in which the direction of the second derivative is known (down) and the level is not. Second, use the second set's cross-check EPS (SEK 2.38, 2.78, 3.29, 3.58) as the figures of record and treat the model's EPS as a basis variant; correct the model's revenue base and working-capital mechanics before the memo (D-01). Third, build the risk discussion around the two channels and their observable leads (Corning, Prysmian, Dura-Line, Clearfield, Dycom, MasTec, spot fibre prices, hyperscaler capex guidance) rather than around the long list of company-specific items, because that is where the predictability of this case will be won or lost.

D-01

Congruence of the answers across the earlier sets

C70

Question asked How congruent are the answers to all the questions in the earlier sets: to what extent do they support and align with each other, rather than contradict or jar?

Judgement. The first set, the second set and the Financial Analyst's model are congruent in direction, mechanism and magnitude to an unusual degree, and the points where they jar are of three kinds, none of which overturns the case: corrections the second set made to the first that I endorse; new evidence from the research commissioned for this set that sharpens one leg of the thesis (US pricing) and softens one risk (the glass constraint on Fiber Solutions); and two mechanical artefacts in the model that make it look more bullish than the Analyst's judgement. The single thesis that all the work supports is this: Hexatronic is a cyclical niche specialist in the second year of an upswing, with a structurally lower cost base and a higher-margin mix than in its last cycle, whose rate of change peaked in Q3 2026, whose 2027 is front-loaded into an easy first half and a hard second half, whose margin plateaus near 11% rather than the 12% target, and whose downside is open while its upside is capped by supply. That thesis is held consistently from the external forces (C-01, C-05, C-10) through the internal forces (C-13, C-21), the economics (C-53, C-75), the management record (C-70) and the risks (C-81), into the second set's inputs (growth 15.3%, 11.5%, 11.5%, 10.3%, 8.2%; EBITA margin 10.1%, 10.9%, 11.3%, 10.9%, 11.0%) and its downside scenario. The congruence is itself informative: it means the predictability question turns on a small number of external variables (D-03), not on unresolved disagreements inside our own work.

Where the sets support each other (the critical chain).

  • Peak rate of change in Q3 2026 (C-06, C-80, group judgement on external forces) becomes the second set's quarterly path (growth 32.7%, 20.7%, 23.1%, 11.4%, 5.3%, 9.9% from Q3 2026 to Q4 2027) and Redeye's independent quarterly path (Q2 2027 growth about 14%). Three sources, one shape.
  • Supply caps the upside (C-05, C-39) becomes 9% organic growth in 2027 against demand indicators above 12% (P-05, P-10), and the AlphaSense driver report (t-d2) finds the same: supply-capped recoveries at non-integrated makers end in deferred revenue more often than lost orders, with a tail of permanent loss where customers migrate to integrated suppliers.
  • The 12% margin target is not credited (C-13, C-55, C-71, the LTIP range of 9.8-11.1%) becomes an 11.3% peak in 2028 and a 10.8% three-year average, inside the upper half of the incentive range; Redeye's 2028 revenue of SEK 10,213m against the SEK 11bn target says the one visible broker does not credit it either.
  • Two correlated external channels (C-81: US carrier spending and hyperscaler spending) become the second set's two swing variables for 2027 (North American Fiber Solutions volume after the refill; the size of Data Center normalisation) and its downside scenario (2027 EPS about SEK 2.1), which matches the first set's own downside range (SEK 2.0-2.3).
  • Management's near-term cues are conservative (C-70) becomes "lean the next two to three quarters up" (P-06); the disclosure report (own-4) confirms the pattern with evidence: cost, pricing and regional cues since late 2025 predicted outcomes, while the 2023-24 demand-recovery cues lagged.
  • The 2029 consensus rests on one broker and a margin step (C-67) becomes a 2029 EPS of SEK 3.58 against SEK 4.18 (P-09); nothing in this set's research supports the consensus step.

Where the sets jar, and what I conclude.

  1. US pricing. The first set (C-02, C-17, C-45) read 2026 US pricing as benign, with industry-wide increases passed through. The research for this set shows that the increases are glass-linked surcharges on fibre cable, while Hexatronic's main US product, duct and microduct, faces the opposite: Orbia reported in Q2 2026 (Dura-Line sales +30%) and again in Q3 2026 that HDPE conduit pricing is under pressure from domestic overcapacity. Hexatronic's US fibre-cable line does not run until 2027, so its US business is duct-heavy. Conclusion: the Fiber Solutions margin recovery is volume absorption on a cut cost base against a price headwind, not volume plus price; this is congruent with the second set's P-04 (price is a pass-through variable, not a profit driver) but it removes a cushion the first set implied. It is the main reason I regard the second set's 2027 Fiber Solutions margin of 9.4% as a ceiling rather than a midpoint once North American growth slows from Q2 2027.
  2. Data Center leverage. Both earlier sets took services as about half of segment sales; the company's own figure is SEK 656m of SEK 1,664m for nine months 2026, 39%. The segment has more product and more operating leverage than the second set's 15-20% incremental margin assumes, which is why the Q3 2026 margin of 16.9% survived the Superior consolidation. The implication is two-sided and congruent with the second set's shape: Q4 2026 normalisation will cost more margin than the second set's 16.2% full-year figure implies if the drop is in product revenue, and the services-dilution path to 13.5% by 2030 is slower than modelled. I treat it as a widening of the cone, not a change of base case.
  3. Which glass is scarce. The first set framed the shortage as a Fiber Solutions constraint. The supply report (own-5) shows standard G.652.D fibre has already fallen from a mid-2026 peak of USD 17-18 to USD 11-13 per fibre-km, while bend-insensitive G.657.A2 fibre for data centres is about USD 35 and MPO ferrules are the binding connector constraint. Conclusion: the growth cap applies most to Data Center products and connectivity (where hyperscalers prioritise availability over price and Hexatronic can pass cost through), less to FTTH duct, and the first relief, lower G.652.D cost, arrives in Fiber Solutions during 2027, about a year before the first set expected. Mild positive for the 2027 gross margin, mild negative for Data Center volume, net neutral to the inputs.
  4. Hudiksvall. The first set expected the revised environmental permit "during 2026"; the Harsh Environment report (own-6) shows it slipped from late 2025 to late 2026 and is still pending, and Redeye books the new line's revenue from 2029. The second set's SEK 100m of subsea sales in 2028 is therefore at risk of a one-year slip; it is about 0.9% of 2028 revenue and SEK 20m of EBITA, inside the cone, so I do not change the input but I list it as a 2028 downside.
  5. Clean FY2026 EPS and the incremental margin. The second set lowered the first set's clean 2026 EPS (SEK 2.25-2.40) to SEK 2.20 and the group incremental margin (30%) to 25-28%; both corrections stand, and the second is reinforced by point 2 above only in the sense that the mix inside Data Center is more product-heavy than assumed.
  6. Acquisition competition. The first set (C-18, C-09) feared private-equity roll-ups lifting entry multiples; the plausibility report (t-d1) cites DNB Carnegie's view that competition for targets under EUR 50m is undemanding and that Lifco and Lagercrantz have kept multiples through cycles. Both can be true: the installer targets Hexatronic wants in US data centres are the ones private equity is consolidating (Wize Solutions, MasTec's purchase of Superior Group at 6.9x, Dycom's Power Solutions at 9.7x), while the small European industrial targets are not. The second set's SEK 400m a year at about 6x is achievable in Europe and defence; it is the US data-centre service deals that may cost more or come slower.

The flagged gaps between the model and the Analyst's cross-check, explained. The one-page summary flags FY2026 free cash flow and EPS in every year. All of the EPS gap and most of the free-cash-flow gap are explained by three things, none of which is a disagreement about the business.
